Queen's Domain review

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Where to start with Queen's Domain? It's tempting to begin with King's Field; after all, FromSoftware's seminal first-person action-RPG is an explicit inspiration for developer Freshly Baked Games. But 40 hours later, it feels like so much more. It's equally tempting to invoke another FromSoftware game: Dark Souls. Queen's Domain owes an obvious debt to that game's celebrated level design: the dazzling interconnectedness of its vertiginous world. But that's not quite right either. The deeper you delve, the more there's something of the spirit of The Legend of Zelda, a touch of Metroidvania too. Until finally you concede that Queen's Domain, with its remarkable world of densely packed secrets, is very much its own.

All this begins with an island. You, Prince Beriik, are a castaway, washed ashore on Neasied. Your father, the king, has disappeared, and your search has brought you, somewhat circuitously, here. As you clamber to your feet and survey the island, it makes an immediately striking impression: all strange peaks and crooked spires; a cramped, chaotic mass of landmarks lost amid the dense undergrowth. Pyramids perch atop steep plateaus; a vast, moss-tinged mausoleum sprawls across the land to one side while the other is dominated by an imposing tower. It's a world made all the stranger by Queen's Domain's retro aesthetic. Here, shimmering low-res textures and low-poly shapes converge to create elaborate spaces and structures that feel slightly at odds with the lo-fi vibe. It's not quite old, not quite new; instead, something of a world lost outside of time. It makes for an enigmatic, disorientating landscape. And when paired with a soundtrack that delights in discordance, as it swings from gentle ambience to pulsing synths, it's a mood that refuses to resolve.

There is so much to say about Neasied. And so much I absolutely will not spoil. But very early impressions don't do it justice at all. As you take your first tentative steps onto the island, presented with a forking path that eventually winds back in on itself, it's easy to mistake Queen's Domain for a first-person Dark Souls - especially if, like me, you're only passingly familiar with King's Field. As in most Soulslikes, early progress is defined by a punishing inch-by-inch crawl from one brutal encounter to the next; where death - via chicken-cricket hybrids, ladybird-rats, and other wonderfully odd things - comes fast and frequently, respawning you back at the last Crystal Monument you visited. And because its wilfully old-school design eschews modern-day niceties such as a combat lock-on or dedicated roll button, there's a looseness to fighting that takes a while to click. But it does, slowly, click.

This is a game that demands you lean into its limitations, encourages you to find ways to make them work. At first, those Souls-honed instincts are likely to be your undoing as you naturally scramble for a dodge button that isn't there. But soon a different sort of pattern emerges: you realise that to avoid a ladybird attack, you simply need to step back - but not so far you don't have time to nip in and strike while your opponent takes a pause. A charging enemy might require a different approach - a well-timed backstep and swivelling sideways strafe to put you in the right place for a counter. And in this way, combat becomes a compelling sort of puzzle as you learn each of the 100+ enemies' little ticks - including rock, paper, scissors-style weaknesses to different weapon types - and figure out how to respond.

There's more to consider. Your melee damage output, for instance, is directly tied to your stamina bar: the more stamina you have, the harder you'll hit; too low and it's a waste of a swing. That forces you to choose your moments carefully, moving out to safety while your bar refills. And there's a charge attack, also linked to your stamina, that does more damage still. Already combat has a distinctively deliberate rhythm, but then come your first throwing knives. These are most immediately useful against enemies ordinarily out of reach - like the laser-spewing foliage dogging your journey early on - but they're equally valuable for luring individual enemies away from the throng. Throwing weapons take a while to return to your pouch, meaning they too need to be deployed with purposes. And while knives are just the start - you'll acquire an increasingly exotic selection of throwables including poison-tipped darts and homing blades - they all fundamentally work the same way.

And the interplay of these various warm-ups and cooldowns creates an increasingly engaging rhythm as you learn to juggle between your variously equipped weapons to maximise their damage potential. You might, for instance, fire off a one-two-three volley of homing darts, then use a magic lantern to toss out a massive fireball that bounces destructively around the room. And it's there, in the ensuing chaos, you'll charge up your blade, letting rip then returning to your darts again. Combat might encourage a methodical pace, but that doesn't mean you can't fight fast, and there are some fascinating synergies among the various weapons, many having their own wonderfully distinct mechanisms. There's a brawling gauntlet that lifts you clean off the ground, for instance, and a vampiric blade capable of stealing an enemy's life force. There are hammers, creatures, and an unexpected mid-game subclass, utilising a third rechargeable power source, that I am definitely not spoiling. Queen's Domain's 100 or so weapons create ample space for experimentation and customisation, and the further from its cautious beginnings you stray, the brisker, the more exhilarating its combat becomes. In my current, late-game form, I'm a whirl of blades and blasts and up-close attacks that feels anything but Soulslike.

Queen's Domain isn't just a nippier sort of game, though. It quickly reveals itself to be a friendlier, more accommodating one. Levelling, for instance, happens passively as you play, meaning every swing feels like it's contributing to your progress. Weapons level independently too, encouraging specialisation. There are no flasks, no accumulated currency you'll need to worry about dropping on death, no subsequent corpse runs. And progress can be saved at any time using the nearest Crystal Monument, meaning that when you respawn, you won't have to grind through the same opponents again. Enemies do eventually reappear of their own accord, so you can't exploit the save system indefinitely. But unlike Souls-style bonfires, Crystal Monuments only repopulate the world when you explicitly tell them to - when you Rest to clear status effects and restore health - giving you considerably more agency. You can decide when to push forward, when to retreat and lock in your progress, and when to reap the risky benefits of a rest and reset. Together, it makes for a breezier, less punishing, more consistently rewarding riff on the traditional Souslike, while never losing that I-can-do-this just-one-more-turn allure.

But as immensely enjoyable as Queen's Domain's combat is, the real draw here, unquestionably, is the world. Neasied is extraordinary. Right from the start, as you pick yourself off the sand and survey the landscape, its enigmatic huddle of peculiar landmarks isn't just intriguing, it's inviting. These odd pyramids, these curious plateaus practically demand to be explored. But how? The answer comes perhaps an hour in when, fancily clad skeleton boss successfully slain, you get your hands on the Holy Sword Endu. It's an immediate boon, far more lethal than the rubbish rusty blade you've been wielding so far. And its secondary attack - a long-range jab that propels you forward - is handy too. It's also your key to unlocking Neasied.

Endu's violent forward momentum can, you see, fling you across previous uncrossable gaps, gradually carrying you further and further as more upgrades reveal themselves. Suddenly, Queen's Domain takes flight, quite literally, becoming something closer to an exploratory sandbox as it adopts the spirited acrobatics of a platform game. And as you experiment with your newfound freedom, the more paths you explore, the island begins to take on a dizzying new form. Neasied, when viewed from up high, reveals itself to be a vastly intricate traversal puzzle. That peculiar structure in the distant ledge? If you can find an appropriate ledge to fling yourself from, it's now yours for the investigating - and from there, one, two, three more tantalising branches are revealed. Neasied is an astonishing piece of design: a ludicrous, elaborate tangle of hidden pathways and secrets, forever folding in and around itself, that's crafted to invite curiosity and then constantly reward.

Freshly Baked Games always feels one step ahead. I can't recall a single moment where my exploratory What-ifs - What if I throw myself this way? What if I do a falling mid-air turn? What if I toss myself down this hole? - didn't bear fruit. Sometimes your prize is a steaming platter of boon-granting food, sometimes it's another bit of increasingly exotic weaponry. Perhaps you'll find a strange NPC with a favour to ask; fish eggs and mysterious skulls; a shopkeeper with a thing for ducks (who, in a typically lovely touch, festoons his store with the goods you're willing to sell), and so many mysterious new puzzle threads to pull. As you fling and probe and push on forward, that thing you've been carrying around for hours - or, conversely, that thing you saw hours ago - suddenly finds new purpose. There's a definite touch of Metroidvania as old areas reveal new truths the further you go. And because there's so little handholding, so little explicit direction, every discovery feels like your own.

But just as you think you're starting to get a handle on Queen's Domain's shape, along comes another surprise: dungeons as vastly intricate as the world outside. You'll navigate a labyrinthine of waterlogged caverns and angry fish where progress involves puzzling past ancient mechanisms. Later, an arcane tower where gargoyles prowl. Here, the convoluted upward route takes circuitous laps among the endless bookshelves, spilling out the windows onto teetering precipices with alarming sea views. Then down, however briefly, to eerie chambers below. Suddenly, the mood is less Soulslike, more Zelda, and your expectations shift again. And still new discoveries lead to new paths and fresh enigmas.

And it keeps on going. As Freshly Baked Games gradually peels back Neasied's layers, it constantly finds new ways to awe: yet more surprises, yet more secrets, your exploratory digressions earning still more intriguing rewards. There comes a point where you think surely you must have seen everything, explored its deepest caverns and most mysterious tombs, but down you go again. And somewhere around the midpoint, Queen's Domain takes a truly audacious turn. There is a - let's call it a tonal lurch for fear of spoilers - that signals another shift. Suddenly, there are new toys to play with, an autumnal landscape so complex in its demands, it genuinely made my brain pulse. And then it's over. Until it's not.

Retro resolution toggle (reduces visual shimmering). Individual volume sliders for master, volume, and effects; headphones mode audio toggle. Screen shake toggle; tap to run toggle. Mouse look sensitivity and acceleration sliders; invert mouse horizontal and vertical look toggles; configurable keyboard and mouse bindings; inert gamepad horizontal and vertical look toggles; vibration intensity slider; stick swap toggle; stick deadzone adjustment slider; configurable gamepad bindings.

There are, it turns out, secrets beneath the secrets; a still more intricate web of interconnected threads spun throughout this already fascinating, impossibly complex world. The Neasied that welcomes you really is just the tip of it all. Exactly when you'll come to this realisation depends how thorough you are in the earlier hours - how willing you are to explore its darker corners and brave its more punishing realms - but for me the before-and-after managed to divide my 40-ish hour playthrough neatly in two. You reawaken with a sense of a quest unfulfilled, a hint that more is required. And so, in lieu of more explicit direction, you set out again, probe deeper, explore further, and slowly the vaguest shape of a new objective - a succession of tiny threads seemingly all leading in the same direction - begins to emerge, tucked along uncharted paths, perhaps, or in suspicious corners, or in item descriptions that suddenly feels like clues.

It's here in this second half - something of a dark mirror to the first, in classic Zelda style - that Queen's Domain takes on a more esoteric air; its web of distantly scattered enigmas requiring real dedication to to unfurl. Why this pot on a stick? Why this book? How do these unlit lanterns relate to this thing, and that thing, and this other thing on the other side of the world?

It's clever stuff, even if navigating its late-game threads can occasionally feel just a little too much, a little too abstruse, and you'll potentially face a lot of needle-in-a-haystack backtracking if you haven't been taking judicious notes as you go. Do I have any other quibbles? There are perhaps too few boss encounters that feel like genuine events. And while combat generally feels fair and balanced, serving as a propulsive drumbeat to the broader exploratory rhythms, there are a few late-game all-the-enemies-at-once-style difficulty spikes that go just a bit too far. But then another 'What if?' and the world responds, and those frustrations melt away.

I remember a time in my youth - back before dataminers and FAQs - when video games felt unknowable. Endless enigmas built from creativity and code. It felt like anything could be hiding in there if only you knew where to look, and it was easy to let your imagination run wild. Queen's Domain is so full of secrets, so full of mystery and wonder, it feels like the sort of game my enthusiastic child-brain might have dreamed up all those years ago. 40 hours in, I've seen an ending, but it feels like I'm still far from through. So many mysteries still linger, so many things remain on my checklist of speculative to-dos. Out in the real-world, I've got a Zelda and a GTA awaiting. But it says everything about Queen's Domain - this brilliant, vast, deep enigma; this absurdly audacious thing - that I'm still not yet ready to let go.

A copy of Queen's Domain was provided for this review by Future Friends Games.

Black Friday PS5 and PS5 Pro deals: See the best deals at Amazon, Best Buy, Walmart, and Target

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you are trying to get your hands on a PlayStation 5 right now, the real win is not a discount — it is simply finding one in stock at standard retail price. Between a global RAM shortage driven by the AI boom, which pushed Sony to hike console prices twice in under a year, and the massive hype around GTA 6, standalone console markdowns are nowhere to be found.

Today, the PS5 Slim Digital Edition starts at $599.99, and the flagship PS5 Pro retails for $899.99. Depending on the model, that means PlayStation 5 hardware actually costs $150 to $200 more today than it did when the console originally launched in 2020.

For weeks, the hype around GTA 6 completely wiped out store shelves across the country — especially for the PS5 Pro. The good news is that major retailers like Walmart, Best Buy, Target, and Amazon are finally restocking standard PS5 models at regular list price.

We recommend paying full sticker price, because third-party marketplace listings are barely worth looking at anyway — the few that are up are mostly refurbished units marked up to nearly double the retail cost, so we left them off our list.

Even at full sticker price, picking up a PS5 or PS5 Pro makes a lot of sense if you are upgrading for GTA 6 or catching up on Sony's massive library of current-gen games.

The standard PlayStation 5 Digital Edition gives you the core current-gen experience in a slim, compact design that fits easily into a media console. You get an 825GB ultra-high-speed SSD that cuts load screens to seconds, 4K TV output, up to 120 fps gameplay on supported titles, and Tempest 3D AudioTech. It also ships with Sony's DualSense wireless controller — featuring haptic feedback and adaptive triggers that add physical resistance when you pull a trigger or drive over rough terrain — and plays more than 8,500 backward-compatible PS4 games with smoother frame rates. Plus, if you ever decide you want to buy physical discs later, you can snap Sony's detachable disc drive right onto the side.

If you want the best possible visuals in Vice City, stepping up to the PlayStation 5 Pro unlocks PlayStation Spectral Super Resolution (PSSR) for sharper AI-upscaled clarity, upgraded GPU performance, and advanced ray tracing. While Rockstar confirmed GTA 6 targets 30 fps across all consoles at launch, the PS5 Pro is the only system that gets exclusive ray-traced shadows and cleaner image upscaling in the game.

Beyond GTA 6, dozens of major PS5 titles run at higher frame rates and sharper resolutions on the Pro, and its massive 2TB SSD easily swallows GTA 6's 212GB install size with plenty of storage left over for the rest of your library.

If you want to lock down a console while shelves still have stock, here is where the PS5 Digital Edition and PS5 Pro are currently available at standard retail price:

Amazon — Along with the standalone PlayStation 5 Digital Edition and PlayStation 5 Pro 2TB available at regular retail price, Amazon also has the PlayStation 5 Digital Edition (825GB) + DualSense Wireless Controller Bundle discounted by roughly $22 — the only actual bundle discount floating around major stores right now.

Best Buy — Both the PlayStation 5 Digital Edition and the PlayStation 5 Pro 2TB are available to order online right now at list price.

Target — You can grab both the PlayStation 5 Digital Edition and the PlayStation 5 Pro 2TB at standard retail price while current restocks last.

Walmart — The PlayStation 5 Digital Edition is back in stock directly from Walmart at regular retail price.

If you don't want to pay 2026's inflated brand-new sticker prices, look straight at PlayStation Direct's certified refurbished lineup. Sony sells official refurbished PS5 and PS5 Pro consoles for up to $100 less than brand-new units ($499.99 for the Digital Edition, $549.99 for the Disc Edition, and $849 for the PS5 Pro), backed by a full one-year manufacturer's warranty. It is the most reliable way to shave real money off the console without gambling on sketchy third-party resellers.

You might also spot occasional game bundles at big-box stores, though availability is hit-or-miss. Even the overpriced bundles and double-the-price third-party listings floating around online are largely sold out, proving just how intense the demand still is.

After watching store shelves sit completely empty less than a week ago, seeing major retailers finally restock both the PS5 and PS5 Pro is a welcome surprise. If you are trying to jump into GTA 6 without paying double to a reseller, grab an official unit at retail price — or check PlayStation Direct's refurbished store — before this wave clears out too.

How to Do Database Migrations Without Downtime at Scale

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Database migrations are deceptively dangerous. A one-line schema change that takes milliseconds on your dev laptop can lock a production table for 10 minutes on a 50-million-row dataset — bringing your application to its knees during peak traffic. The problem isn't the migration itself. It's how most teams approach it.

This article covers the patterns that actually work: how to restructure schema changes so they're safe to run against live traffic, with real code for both Python (Alembic) and Go.

Most relational databases (Postgres, MySQL) require table-level or row-level locks for certain DDL operations. The worst offenders:

PostgreSQL has improved significantly — ADD COLUMN with a stable default is a metadata-only operation in PG 11+. But many operations still require a full table scan, and the moment you add a constraint or modify a column type, you're back in dangerous territory.

The root issue: your application and your migration tool race against each other. While the migration holds a lock, incoming queries queue up. If the migration takes 30 seconds and your connection pool is 50 connections, you've effectively taken down the service.

The solution is to decompose breaking changes into multiple non-breaking steps. This is called the expand-contract pattern (sometimes called "parallel change"):

Each phase is independently deployable. No phase requires a table lock that blocks reads. The key: at no point does the application depend on a structure that doesn't exist yet.

Renaming a column is the canonical example. The naive ALTER TABLE users RENAME COLUMN name TO full_name is safe in Postgres but your application code will break the moment it deploys if it references name before the migration runs — or vice versa.

Here's the expand-contract approach with Alembic:

Deploy your application to write to both columns and read from the new one with a fallback:

Once every application instance is on the new code and the backfill is confirmed complete, run Phase 3:

This three-step process takes more calendar time (you need a deploy between each phase), but zero seconds of downtime. For tables with more than a few thousand rows, it's the only safe approach.

The batch loop above works, but naive batch updates create their own problems. On a 100M-row table, looping with LIMIT will cause full sequential scans on each iteration. Processing in primary-key ranges is faster and more predictable:

Two adjustments that matter at scale: commit after each batch (not inside a single giant transaction), and add a sleep between batches proportional to your replication lag tolerance. If you have read replicas serving traffic, monitor pg_stat_replication lag and pause the backfill if it climbs above your SLA threshold.

This approach is also audit-friendly: each committed batch is a discrete, recoverable unit. For environments where regulatory compliance requires row-level change tracking, processing in bounded ranges makes reconciliation straightforward.

Adding an index is frequently overlooked as a migration risk. Standard CREATE INDEX acquires a full table lock. The fix in PostgreSQL:

The CONCURRENTLY option builds the index in multiple passes without holding a table lock, at the cost of slightly longer build time. The catch: it cannot run inside a transaction. In Alembic, you must opt out of the automatic transaction wrapper:

The IF NOT EXISTS guard makes the migration idempotent — safe to retry if it's interrupted mid-build.

The expand-contract pattern requires strict sequencing: migrations before code, always. A fast-fail check at startup enforces this in Go:

Call this in main() before binding the HTTP server. A startup crash with a clear error message beats a runtime panic at 2 AM. Pair it with a readiness probe in Kubernetes so the deployment stalls rather than routing traffic to instances running against an incompatible schema.

Zero-downtime migrations are not about clever database tricks — they're about discipline in how you structure schema changes. The expand-contract pattern adds steps, but those steps are each individually safe to deploy.

For a broader set of database hardening rules — covering access controls, backup verification, and privilege auditing — the free security checklists at AYI NEDJIMI Consultants cover the operational side in detail.

How to get Codex Desktop worktrees working again after an SSH reconnect

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If Codex Desktop stops creating remote worktrees after an SSH reconnect, fully quit and reopen the app. That restored worktree creation in my tests. If idle SSH drops are what trigger the problem for you, client-side keepalives may help you run into it less often.

I reproduced this on macOS with Codex Desktop 26.1117.41037 and Codex CLI 0.171.0 on the SSH host.

After reconnecting, Desktop accepted the request and returned a temporary clientThreadId, but no new chat or Git worktree appeared. The host still showed Connected, and I could still list its chat history. That’s the clue I found most useful: the connection looked healthy during ordinary reads, while worktree creation failed.

In my tests, restarting Desktop restored worktree creation. Disconnecting and reconnecting from Settings → Connections while the app stayed open reproduced the failure.

On the Mac running Codex Desktop, first check the settings SSH already uses for your host. Replace my-devbox with the SSH alias you use in Desktop.

This prints the effective settings without opening a connection. If serveraliveinterval is 0, SSH protocol keepalives are off. To enable them, open ~/.ssh/config on your Mac and add these two lines inside the existing Host my-devbox block:

Rerun the check. You should see these values, in either order:

SSH will send a check after 30 seconds without hearing from the server. If the server stays unresponsive, SSH will disconnect after about three minutes. This can help when a router or firewall drops idle connections. OpenSSH keepalive settings

Fully quit and reopen Desktop so the next SSH connection uses the new settings. I verified that SSH picked up both values and Desktop opened a fresh connection. Whether the keepalives reduce disconnects during normal use is still unverified. If worktree creation fails after a future reconnect, fully restart Desktop again.

Running a crypto checkout under /coldtill behind the nginx you already have

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Most self-hosted apps assume they own a hostname. Give them pay.example.com, let them get their own certificate, and everything is simple. But sometimes you already have a box running nginx for example.com, with TLS sorted, an auth layer in front of your other admin pages, and no appetite for yet another subdomain and certificate to look after.

Coldtill, the self-hosted crypto payment gateway I work on, has supported running under a path like /coldtill since v1.2.1 in October. This post is the setup in full, the two mistakes that break it, and the reason I'd still tell most people to use a subdomain. That last part is at the end, and it matters.

There are two common ways to put an app under a prefix, and they fail in opposite directions.

On a fresh server, give the installer the full URL with its path:

The docs show this with a Pro token, but the flags are the same. (On mainnet you also need SMTP settings for payout approval emails, so read the install guide before taking real money.)

When the site URL has a path, coldtill init sets up the bundled Caddy differently. It listens on plain HTTP on 127.0.0.1:8080 instead of taking ports 80 and 443, so it doesn't fight nginx. To move an existing install, set these in deploy/.env (that's /opt/coldtill/.env on a one-line install) and run docker compose up -d:

This goes inside the server block that already serves example.com on 443:

Three details are worth slowing down for.

No trailing slash on proxy_pass. In nginx, proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:8080; passes the request URI through unchanged, while proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:8080/; replaces the matched /coldtill/ with /. That one character is mistake number one from the list above. A rewrite that strips the prefix does the same damage.

One value in X-Forwarded-For. It's tempting to use $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for out of habit. The docs deliberately set it to $remote_addr, the single address nginx saw. Coldtill only trusts the rightmost X-Forwarded-For hop, and by default its Caddy only believes forwarded headers from private address ranges, which is where nginx connects from. That matters because the rate limits work per client IP: login is limited to 10 attempts a minute per IP and per email, and the merchant API to 600 requests a minute per IP. If every request looks like it comes from 127.0.0.1, your customers and your attackers share one bucket.

If something else sits in front of nginx, such as a CDN, $remote_addr will be the CDN's address, not the visitor's. Fix that in nginx with its real IP module before it gets passed on, so the one value you forward is the real one.

client_max_body_size 4m. The Caddy config in Coldtill's docs caps request bodies at 4 MB too, so this just keeps the two layers agreeing on the limit.

Your customers need the checkout, and your shop's server needs the merchant API. Everything else under the prefix is the dashboard. Here's what has to be reachable without your admin login:

Miss /api/checkout/ and the checkout page will load but can't fetch the payment it's meant to show. Miss /branding/ and the customer sees a checkout without your logo. Both look like bugs in the gateway when they're actually in the proxy config.

This is the main reason to run under a path at all: you already protect /admin or similar with something, and you want the payment dashboard behind the same thing. Protect the whole prefix, then carve out the open paths with a regex location:

This works because of how nginx picks a location. It finds the longest matching prefix location, then checks regex locations in order, and a matching regex wins unless the prefix location was marked ^~. So don't add ^~ to the /coldtill/ block, or the open paths will quietly start demanding a login and your checkout will break for every customer.

Your layer sits in front of Coldtill's own, not instead of it. Login still needs the password and the two-factor code, and anything that moves money still asks for a fresh code.

Two things the docs spell out, and that are easy to break with a "helpful" proxy setting.

Every dashboard request that changes something must be JSON and carry an Origin header whose host matches the site URL's host. So keep proxy_set_header Host $host;, and don't let nginx rewrite Origin. If saving a setting in the dashboard fails while everything else works, check this first.

A few things stay where they were, which is worth knowing before you go looking for them under the prefix:

On the shop side, the base URL now includes the path. With the TypeScript SDK:

A quick smoke test once it's up: create a test payment and check that the returned url contains /coldtill/pay/. Open it in a private window, where you're not signed in to your admin layer. The checkout should load with your logo. Then open https://example.com/coldtill/ in the same window. It should hit your auth layer, not the Coldtill login page.

Here's the part I promised. A path is not a security boundary.

What limits the damage is the design underneath. A two-factor code is still needed before payouts, API keys and other sensitive actions. API payouts only go to address-book entries. And customer deposits sit in contracts that can only send to your cold wallet, so even full control of the server can't redirect them. But "the blast radius is limited" isn't the same as "it's fine".

So the rule from the docs is the one I'd follow: run Coldtill under a path only beside an app you trust as much as Coldtill itself. A static site you control, sure. A big plugin-heavy CMS with a dozen third-party scripts on every page, no. Give it its own subdomain, which the browser keeps separate, and protect the dashboard with Tailscale or a Cloudflare rule instead.

If you want to try this on a spare box, the free edition, install script and checksums are in the self-hosted gateway repo on GitHub. The "Run Coldtill under a path" section of the domains guide on coldtill.com has the same config with a bit more context. I'd do a testnet install first: get the nginx side right while nothing real is at stake.

What vatnode Logs About Your Account, and How Long

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 23 • Originally published at vatnode.dev

Originally published at vatnode.dev. The version on vatnode.dev is the canonical source — refer to it for the latest content.

vatnode records account creation, every sign-in, every use of ‘Sign out other devices’, the creation of a live API key and the revocation of a key, and shows the latest 20 of those events on your Security page. For sign-ins recorded with an IP address, the IP and user agent are removed after 30 days, and the rows are deleted after one year.

Two logs feed the activity view: a security events log for account creation, sign-ins and signing out other devices, and an audit log for API key changes.

The audit log also records account deletion, billing events such as a cancellation, and changes vatnode support makes on your behalf. Those entries are kept on the same schedule but do not appear in the activity card.

A sign-in is logged the same way whether you used Google, GitHub or an emailed sign-in link or code.

API calls are not sign-ins either: a VAT check made with an API key creates no security event. The check stores the client user agent and no IP address.

You can, on the Security page of the dashboard, in the ‘Recent activity’ card. It lists the latest 20 events, newest first, with the user agent shortened to something like ‘Chrome 120 on Mac’. It is not the full history.

category is auth or api_key, and ip is null when no address was captured. The dashboard shows that case as ‘IP unknown’.

For security events, the 30-day removal applies to sign-ins recorded with an IP address. A daily job at 03:00 UTC applies both periods. They cover these two logs only; they are not a general statement about every IP address vatnode ever sees. If you delete your account, the security events and audit entries are de-linked from it when the account record is purged. That happens 30 days after the deletion request, or later if a payment still has to be reconciled first.

The new sign-in email has the subject ‘New sign-in to your vatnode account’. It goes out when a sign-in comes from an IP address that does not appear in your account’s sign-ins over the previous 30 days. The lookback matches the retention, so the comparison runs against the addresses vatnode still holds. It is not sent when no earlier sign-in is on record, such as your very first sign-in, where the welcome email goes out instead. The email shows the time in UTC, the IP address and the raw device and browser string, with a button to your API keys.

Creating a live key or revoking a key also sends an email, with the key’s label, its last four characters and the IP address. That email is covered in structuring vatnode API keys across teams.

vatnode has no passwords. Whoever controls your email inbox, or your Google or GitHub account, can sign in as you, so start there.

For how check data is stored, see GDPR and VAT data storage. Every vatnode account has the Security page, Free included. After you register, the ‘Recent activity’ card shows your account creation and first sign-in straight away. How keys are issued and sent is in the authentication docs, and pricing lists how many keys each plan allows.

Account creation, every sign-in, every use of ‘Sign out other devices’, the creation of a live API key and the revocation of a key. Sign-ins and sign-outs of other devices store the time, IP address and user agent. Key events store the IP address, and a creation also stores the key label. Logouts, failed sign-ins and requests for a sign-in link or code are not recorded.

For sign-ins recorded with an IP address, the IP and user agent are removed after 30 days, and the event row itself is deleted after one year. In the audit log that holds API key changes, the IP is removed after 30 days and the rows are deleted after one year. These periods apply to those two logs only.

It sends one when a sign-in comes from an IP address that has not appeared in your account sign-ins during the previous 30 days. The trigger is the IP address, not the device, so the same browser on a new network triggers it and a new browser on a known network does not. It is not sent when no earlier sign-in is on record, such as your very first sign-in.

Secure the email inbox and the Google or GitHub account you sign in with, because vatnode has no passwords, then press ‘Sign out other devices’ on the Security page and write to mail@vatnode.dev. Revoke any live API key you did not create, delete webhooks you did not add or whose URL you did not set, check monitoring subscriptions and the alert email address, the requester VAT and your plan and billing details, and regenerate the test key.

Ad Platform Can't Handle Traffic Spikes? What to Fix First and Who Can Help

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 23 • Originally published at ambrain.org

At traffic spikes, queues and retries make an ad platform answer after the deadline. Measure the peak and cut that late work before adding servers.

If your ad platform fails at traffic spikes, the engineers who can help are the ones who measure it during a real peak and then fix it in a set order. They stop work that will finish after the deadline, limit retries and the traffic the platform accepts, and move budget and frequency counters off the request path. Then they add capacity before the peaks you can predict. Code is rewritten last, and only where the measurements point.

The short answer: in real-time bidding, a bid that misses the exchange's deadline is lost. At a peak, queues, retries and shared budget counters make more bids miss it. Whoever you hire should ask for your timeouts per partner and queue depth per service at the busiest minute before proposing more servers or a rewrite.

A traffic spike shows different symptoms in each part of an ad platform:

Timeouts and empty slots show up during the peak. Event and budget problems can stay hidden until delayed events reach reports and billing.

In OpenRTB, the IAB Tech Lab protocol for real-time bidding, the exchange can state the deadline in the request itself: the “maximum time in milliseconds the exchange allows for bids to be received including Internet latency to avoid timeout”. Google's Authorized Buyers documentation says the deadline typically ranges from 80 to 1000 ms. Google requires 85 percent of responses to arrive within it, as seen from the trading location, and throttles bidders that cannot consistently achieve this. A bidder that slows down at a peak loses the late auctions and can then receive less traffic.

Near capacity, a service starts to queue requests. Google's Site Reliability Engineering (SRE) book notes that “queued requests consume memory and increase latency”, and that servers spend resources on requests that will miss their deadline anyway. Unless the code checks the deadline, a request that has waited too long is still processed in full and its answer discarded.

When a call to a database, a cache or a partner times out, the caller tries again, and the retries arrive when the system can least absorb them. The SRE book gives the arithmetic of a retry storm: “100 QPS of retries in the first second leads to 200 QPS, then to 300 QPS, and so on.”

An exchange or SSP sends each request to many bidders, and the auction either waits for the slowest answer or closes without it. Jeffrey Dean and Luiz André Barroso of Google put numbers on this in Communications of the ACM in 2013. In their example, each server typically answers in 10 ms but takes a second on one request in a hundred. A request that must collect answers from 100 such servers in parallel then takes more than a second 63 percent of the time. An auction does not wait that long. By the same calculation, if each of 100 bidders is late on one request in a hundred, about 63 percent of auctions close with at least one answer missing.

Autoscaling that reacts to load adds copies of a service only after it has measured that load. Kubernetes' Horizontal Pod Autoscaler, for example, checks the load every 15 seconds by default and adds new copies in limited steps. Each new copy then has to start, pass its checks and fill its caches, and the SRE book notes that processes are often slower right after starting than in steady state. A spike measured in seconds can end before the new capacity carries real traffic.

Measure these for the busiest minutes of a real peak:

Put these numbers for your last big peak on one page. It is the brief for anyone you hire and the baseline for every fix.

Work in this order, from cheap changes that stop wasted work to expensive ones that add capacity or replace code, and measure again after each step.

A full rewrite is rarely the right first step. It takes engineers away from feature work until the new code carries production traffic, and without peak measurements nobody can say which part to rewrite. Rewrite one component when the numbers keep pointing at it after the cheaper fixes, for example a bidder whose slowest responses come from pauses in its runtime. Replace it behind the same interface and compare the same peak numbers before and after.

Help for an ad platform that fails at traffic spikes comes from five places, and each covers a different part of the problem:

Ask these questions before signing. The answers help show whether a firm has done this kind of work:

amBrain is a Yerevan, Armenia software engineering company.

amBrain diagnoses slow systems in trading and ad tech: the running platform is measured end to end and the report names where the time goes. amBrain takes over projects that stalled with another team and brings them to production.

In AdTech, amBrain works on DSP development, real-time bidding platforms, and ad exchange engineering.

amBrain builds supply-side platforms (SSP) for publishers. amBrain builds ad servers: targeting, frequency capping and reporting. amBrain builds event analytics pipelines for ad tech: collection, processing and reporting of impression and click events. amBrain builds ML inference inside the bidder: the model decides the bid within the auction window.

amBrain built RTBBidder, a demand-side platform, for a client. amBrain works in three formats: full delivery, a dedicated team, or engineers embedded in your team. The client keeps full ownership of the product and the code, except amBrain's reusable components.

This article is not a case study. It does not claim that amBrain has fixed peak-traffic failures on any client's ad platform, and it gives no numbers about RTBBidder and no prices or timelines.

If your platform failed at its last peak, start with one page of measurements from that peak. Send it to every firm you consider, amBrain included, and compare how each one proposes to use it.

Qualcomm Is Down 42% and SK hynix Has Plunged 48% Amid the AI Sell-Off. Which Is the Better Stock to Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Artificial intelligence is reshaping semiconductor demand, but these two chipmakers offer investors distinctly different opportunities as the industry heads into 2027.

By RJ Fulton – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:00AM EST

The recent sell-off across the semiconductor industry has created potential buying opportunities as concerns surrounding artificial intelligence (AI) valuations and tightening financial conditions weigh on technology stocks. Qualcomm (NASDAQ: QCOM) and SK hynix (KRX: 000660) have both suffered substantial declines from their recent highs, raising the question of which company offers better value heading into 2027.

Qualcomm specializes in wireless connectivity and processors used in smartphones, automotive systems, and other connected devices. SK hynix, meanwhile, has established itself as a leading supplier of high-bandwidth memory, a critical component of the advanced processors powering AI data centers.

Although both companies stand to benefit from continued investment in AI, their contrasting growth prospects, financial performance, and valuations present two distinctly different investment opportunities following the recent semiconductor sell-off.

Qualcomm's products power smartphones, automotive systems, and a growing range of connected devices. The company maintains established relationships with major smartphone manufacturers, including Apple (NASDAQ: AAPL) and Samsung (OTC: SSNLF), while expanding into automotive computing, data centers, and AI infrastructure.

For the 12 months ended September 27, 2026, Qualcomm generated approximately $43.1 billion in revenue, representing a 2.6% decline from the previous year. Nevertheless, the company remained highly profitable, reporting approximately $13.6 billion in net income, equivalent to a net profit margin of 31.6%.

Qualcomm also maintains a relatively strong financial position, with a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.5 and a current ratio of 1.9. For fiscal 2026, the company generated approximately $8.7 billion in free cash flow, providing substantial flexibility to fund research and development, pursue growth opportunities, and return capital to shareholders. However, stock-based compensation accounted for approximately 31% of operating cash flow, an important consideration when evaluating the quality of its cash generation.

SK hynix is a leading semiconductor manufacturer specializing in advanced memory chips used in smartphones, computers, and artificial intelligence data centers. The company has become a critical supplier to the AI industry, where demand for increasingly powerful processors has driven substantial investment in high-performance memory technologies.

Financial performance has accelerated considerably alongside this demand. For the 12 months ended September 30, 2026, SK hynix generated approximately $201.4 billion in revenue, representing year-over-year growth of 220.3%. Net income reached approximately $141.2 billion, equivalent to a net profit margin of 70.1%. However, a portion of this profitability reflects nonoperating gains rather than recurring earnings from semiconductor manufacturing.

SK hynix also maintains a relatively strong financial position, with a debt-to-equity ratio of approximately 0.2 and a current ratio of 1.9 as of December 2025. During fiscal 2025, the company generated approximately $18.5 billion in free cash flow.

Qualcomm faces growing competitive pressure as major customers, particularly Apple, develop proprietary modem technologies to reduce their dependence on third-party suppliers. The company also faces geopolitical risks stemming from U.S.-China trade restrictions, as well as ongoing legal and regulatory challenges related to its patent licensing practices.SK hynix faces a different set of risks, primarily related to the cyclical nature of the memory semiconductor market. Although surging demand for AI infrastructure has supported its growth, an increase in memory production capacity or slowdown in data center spending could put significant pressure on profit margins.

SK hynix appears cheaper based on Forward P/E, while Qualcomm offers a lower P/S ratio, which measures market cap divided by sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

From a valuation standpoint, SK hynix appears considerably more attractive, trading at just 4.4 times forward earnings compared to Qualcomm's 16.7. Yet their price-to-sales ratios are nearly identical, at 4.2 and 4.0, respectively. In other words, investors are paying roughly the same amount per dollar of revenue, but substantially less per dollar of SK hynix's expected earnings. However, its unusually low earnings multiple also reflects the cyclical nature of the memory industry and uncertainty surrounding the sustainability of its profits.

Following the recent semiconductor sell-off, I favor SK hynix heading into 2027. The company has significant exposure to the growing AI infrastructure market, where demand for advanced memory continues to expand. Its strong cash generation and relatively modest debt burden provide financial flexibility to fund additional manufacturing capacity, while the sharp decline in its share price presents a potentially attractive entry point for long-term investors.

Qualcomm remains a profitable, financially sound business with opportunities beyond its established smartphone operations. However, its more mature core business and comparatively higher earnings valuation make it less compelling following the recent downturn. Although SK hynix faces greater exposure to volatile memory prices and fluctuations in AI infrastructure spending, I believe its stronger growth prospects, attractive valuation, and competitive position in advanced memory offer a more favorable risk-reward profile for investors willing to tolerate additional volatility.

Sandisk Just Split Its Stock 8-for-1. Is It Time to Buy?

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sandisk stock is incredibly cheap right now.

By Keithen Drury – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:00AM EST

If you're wondering why Sandisk (SNDK -16.05%) just saw its share price get a huge cut, it's not time to panic. Although the stock is valued at an eighth of what it was, that was on purpose.

Sandisk enacted an 8-for-1 stock split on Nov. 19, which replaced one share with eight — each of them costing an eighth as much as a pre-split share. This is a mostly cosmetic effect, but it does let the company issue stock options to employees at more reasonable prices.

With most brokerages offering access to fractional shares, stock price really doesn't matter much to investors. What matters more is the business behind it. For a company to justify a split, its share price must have increased significantly. That only happens to companies that are doing well, and Sandisk fits that bill.

Sandisk makes memory chips, which are in short supply. The artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure build-out has consumed all available production capacity for memory, with different effects on suppliers and clients.

For Sandisk, the lack of supply means it can charge premium prices for its products. None of its input costs have changed significantly, so this is almost pure profit for Sandisk. This has shown up in a big way in its most recent results, with profit margins skyrocketing in recent quarters.

SNDK Profit Margin (Quarterly) data by YCharts

A 73% profit margin is absurd, and it shows just how profitable Sandisk is.

While Sandisk and its investors are thrilled about this, not everyone is. Consumers who want to buy laptops, phones, or external storage devices face prices several times higher than they were just a couple of years ago. The AI hyperscalers are experiencing the same thing, paying a premium to secure memory for data centers.

With the AI build-out expected to last several more years, it's unlikely that Sandisk and its peers' supply constraint issues are fixed. Although many in the industry are planning production expansion (Sandisk is too), there are questions about whether that will be enough to meet demand. After all, there is a huge risk of overbuilding production capacity.

Sandisk and its memory chip peers are cyclical companies, which means their businesses ebb and flow. The AI build-out is the largest demand surge this industry has ever seen, and they were woefully underprepared for it in terms of capacity.

But for most of their existence, memory chip companies have significantly underutilized the capacity at their disposal. The AI build-out won't last forever, so Sandisk must be careful not to overbuild; otherwise, it could find itself in a rough spot when the build-out slows.

However, the build-out may not wrap up for several more years, so some capacity investments are warranted. Still, the market is pricing Sandisk's stock like it's going to collapse tomorrow, and that's a perfect opportunity to buy.

Very few companies ever trade at a forward valuation like this.

Sandisk shares go for a mere 3.4 times forward earnings. That is an incredibly cheap valuation, and it conveys the market's message that it doesn't think Sandisk will be able to maintain its earnings growth.

However, this flies in the face of what major AI companies have said, so something has to give. I think it's more likely than not that the market is misvaluing Sandisk's stock, and today it represents an excellent buying opportunity. I think that Sandisk has a ton of potential, and with it being an important component supplier for the AI build-out, the stock is well worth buying.

Delicious food, great golf and a few days in paradise

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As a golf writer, I get to play at some pretty incredible courses. The USGA brought me out to Winged Foot to check out its new app, and it was one of the most incredible golf experiences I've ever had.

But when I received an invite to Mexico to experience Gourmet and Golf at Punta Mita, I almost fell out of my chair with excitement. I've never been to Mexico, and I've never played golf outside of the United States, so going was a no-brainer.

And I made the right decision in more ways than one. Yes, the golf was great, but this event completely changed my life for the better. Seriously.

I attended the 15th anniversary of Punta Mita's Gourmet and Golf, which led the resorts (St. Regis Punta Mita Resort and the Four Seasons Resort Punta Mita) to go a little crazy with the number and quality of chefs. But any year you go, the food and golf will be incredible, and I personally can't wait to see how good the event is for its 20th anniversary.

If you decide to attend, you're looking at $3,599 for a 3-night package and $4,399 for the four-night (I did three, and it was enough time to play two rounds of golf and eat until I felt I might pop). This includes a beautiful place to stay, all ultra-high-end meals, participation in the Punta Mita Golf Cup over 2 days at both Jack Nicklaus Signature Golf Courses, transportation to and from the airport, and more.

It's not cheap, but the value is there — how often do you get to indulge in three days of top-of-the-line food and two days of premium Jack Nicklaus Signature Golf (with one day playing out as a two-person scramble and one as a best ball event)?

This feels weird to say for me after 40 years of being overwhelmingly picky, but I was a super picky eater. Saying "was" just doesn't feel right, but going to Gourmet and Golf actually helped me break out of my picky-eating habits.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Sure, it's only been a couple of weeks, and I have to keep the momentum going by continuing to try new foods, but I feel confident saying I was a picky eater, not that I am one.

I figured that since Gourmet and Golf features some of the world's best chefs, it would be the perfect chance to let my guard down and try whatever was put in front of me. So I did. I tried foods that would normally make me recoil in fear. I ate caviar, tacos and things I can't even pronounce; things I don't even remember. I tried shrimp, lobster and other foods I've had in the past and didn't like. I confirmed I still don't like them, but I tried them without hesitation.

It was like a food blur, and just about everything I tried was great. I counted for the first couple of days of the trip, but eventually the whirlwind of new foods became too much. I'd guess I tried at least 40 new foods.

If you're a picky eater like me, Gourmet and Golf could change your approach to food if you let it. And if you're not a picky eater (like my girlfriend Andria, who accompanied me on the trip), then you're in for a treat, because the food is some of the best you can get. There's a reason these are Michelin-starred chefs — they know what they're doing.

Okay, so the food was good. It changed my life and helped me overcome my fear of trying new foods. Good for me, right? But how were the Nicklaus-designed Bahia and Pacifico golf courses?

I keep track of my golf course ratings on a cool app called Eden. In it, Winged Foot West is my number one course — it gets 10 on my rating scale. Now, though, it has another course nipping at its heels, with the Pacifico course at Punta Mita getting a 9.9. And it's close — while ranking the courses, I actually considered putting Punta Mita above Winged Foot West based primarily on the blend of incredible vistas and the overall quality of the golf.

In the end, the history and legacy offered by Winged Foot gave it a slight edge, but it's close. And the Bahia course isn't far behind, coming in as the 7th best course I've ever played (beaten by Winged Foot West, Tamarack Country Club, Pacifico, Country Club of Waterbury, Scottsdale National and PGA National). Between the two courses, Punta Mita is clearly a destination with golfers in mind.

To give you an idea of some of the other courses with the Nicklaus pedigree, we're talking about The Saint Andrews Golf Club, Great Bear Golf Club, Bayside, Champion Course at PGA National, Harbour Town Golf Links, Nicklaus Course at PGA West and the list goes on and on. Punta Mita's golf courses come with some serious pedigree, and they live up to the challenge for which Nicklaus is known.

Which would explain why I shot a 115 during the best ball portion of the tournament.

These are hard golf courses designed to test every aspect of your golf game with massive, intelligently placed bunkers, narrow fairways, undulating greens, false fronts and all the other tricks that rack up strokes.

But nothing about the design feels haphazard or just there to make things difficult. It's all built to reward smart course management and good shots — something I was apparently incapable of during this particular trip (to give you some perspective, I typically shoot in the mid-to-upper 90s, so I shot almost 20 strokes more than normal).

You know what? I don't care. My partner didn't care. It's hard to care what you shoot when you're surrounded by such beautiful scenery, like you are on both the Bahia and Pacifico courses at Punta Mita. And when the round was over, I knew I had some of the best culinary delights I've ever experienced waiting for me (and strategically placed around the two courses).

It doesn't matter what you shoot; you will have a good time at Gourmet and Golf. Just ask my partner — she didn't even play golf and can't stop talking about going back for another year.

Anthropic researcher tunes chess software to turn a 200 MHz Pentium Pro into a behemoth that would handily defeat Kasparov and Carlsen

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A mere 200 MHz chip with hyper-optimized software is enough to destroy all humans.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Readers with back pain probably recall the much-discussed 1996 chess challenge, wherein the world champion Gerry Kasparov faced off against IBM's Deep Blue chess-optimized computer. The nascent clanker lost the match with a 4-2 total, but still stunned the world by being the first piece of electric sand to win a single game against a grandmaster. Modern chess engines will utterly destroy any human, but Anthropic engineer Nicholas Carlini wondered if granting old hardware new bytes would give the machine a better chance. Spoiler: yes, indeed it would.

To understand the task, here's a quick primer on chess scoreboards. A player is rated worldwide with an Elo system (first devised for chess), wherein they lose or gain points in proportion to the difference to their opponent's rating — meaning that winning a match against Magnus Carlsen would net you a lot of points versus defeating me, who only learned about the en passant rule a handful of years ago.

At his prime, Kasparov ranked about 2800 points. Given that Deep Blue eventually barely won against him in a subsequent rematch, that is a good starting point for any. Contemporary chess engines like Stockfish have an estimated Elo of about 3,300, far higher than any human — guaranteeing it'll win most matches against a human.

Nicholas Carlini got hold of a 1995-era machine, packing a Pentium Pro CPU with all of its 200 MHz of clock speed, and 64 MB of RAM. For his first experiment, he grabbed Crafty, a 1996 chess engine, and measured how well it would do. It came in at 2,000 Elo with 1 second allotted, and 2,400 @ 10s. Running that software on a Ryzen 7 resulted in a 2,600 rating — good, but not enough.

Since Crafty's source code is available, he got Claude to mod it with modern features, and eventually reached a ceiling of 3,000 Elo @ 10s per move. That's quite an impressive feat already, especially considering that the lone Pentium Pro represented but a small fraction of the hardware that Deep Blue had. An Elo delta of +200 means this iteration would defeat Kasparov three out of every four games, while a +400 delta means a victory nine out of ten times.

Carlini then pulled out the big gun: Stockfish. He had to ask Claude to modify it to use only 32-bit arithmetic instead of 64-bit, and limit its workspace to the measly 64 MB of RAM, minus whatever the system harness took. Right off the bat, the figures were revealing: 2,500 @ 1s, and 3000 @ 10s.

The engineer performed a few optimization passes, and eventually got 2,850 @ 1s; 3,100 @ 10s; and almost 3,200 @ 30s — enough to put every human in their place, especially in a grandmaster tournament setting where there's no fixed time per move. You can play the bot using the link on the respective blog post. We'd wish you the best of luck, but there's no such concept in chess.

Goodbye, World: The End of Programmers

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Becoming a programmer was, for me, a compromise. Midway through my junior year of college, it became clear that, as an international student from Korea, I likely wouldn’t be able to stay long after graduation unless I changed my major from philosophy to a STEM field that made me eligible for a visa extension. The obvious choice was computer science, which was already my minor.

The switch came with a sense of shame, which in retrospect had a whiff of melodrama. I suppose I was afflicted by what Ian McEwan once called “modern forms of snobbery” in describing a character who, after her bookish college education, came to consider “a man with a degree in chemistry incomplete as a human being.” Chemistry, computer science—same diff. It’s safe to say I did not have many friends in the CS department.

But I don’t think my squeamishness was entirely misplaced. In the post-Facebook-IPO ferment of the 2010s, this country’s computer science departments were newly flush with a certain breed of striver who in another era would’ve gone to Wall Street. My theory is that many in America who acquired a hostility toward technology acquired it by way of a hostility toward these upstarts. That is to say, they aren’t so much anti-technology as anti-technologist.

In any event, I half-assed my way through a good many CS assignments, which meant I was never in danger of going to grad school for AI. But by virtue of a robust public math education in Korea, my entry into the subject was, mercifully, not too bumpy. If anything, theoretical computer science and distributed systems seemed like a continuation of philosophy.

But I could not dispel the sense that programming was a frivolous waste of energy. Every line of code I had to read, every line of code I had to write, was a theft of the time I could’ve spent reading something I cared about. Perhaps it didn’t help that my first two languages were Java (hideous and tedious) and C (admirable but unforgiving). To this day, Java code is like a poisoned madeleine that brings back unwelcome memories.

After college, my visa dictated that I take a job directly tied to my major, and I found a tech company willing to sponsor my green card. The issue was, it could be any number of years until I got the green card—four, it’d turn out—so I figured I might as well find a way to make programming pleasurable.

The first couple of months on the job were joyless. I worked at a health care startup, which I thought gave me a serviceable sense of moral cover against the ongoing techlash and a way to indemnify myself later as a writer. But I liked my coworkers, some of whom also harbored artistic ambitions, and after work we’d regularly adjourn to literary events at the McNally Jackson, a block from our office.

Then, one day, I discovered a language called Haskell. I was tasked with rewriting a code base using a library that borrowed concepts from it. My vague impression of Haskell had been that it was a difficult language involving a branch of mathematics called category theory. There was something seductive about the intimidating aura around it, and when I tried my hand at writing basic programs, the language was indeed challenging, but appealingly so.

I started lurking in Haskell forums where researchers in the field of programming languages—“PL,” as it’s known—flaunted their lavish command of the language. PL researchers might as well be the poets of computer science: a small community of attentive monomaniacs who engaged in a largely unprofitable enterprise—a rarity in computer science—with all the attendant beef and infighting. The unprofitability meant that, unlike AI, the field had an incorruptible dignity to it.

Soon, to my surprise, I found myself programming outside work. After months of tinkering with Haskell, I discovered a book titled Writing a Compiler in Go. Turns out you could even invent a language yourself. I made a few toy languages, useless in practice but invaluable for demystifying how programming languages work.

There was also an oblique way coding could help writing. In Draft No. 4, John McPhee, the journalist’s journalist, says that he still uses an ancient text editor called Kedit, recommended to him in 1984 by a programmer at Princeton. (“I have never used a word processor,” he writes.) When I switched to a text editor myself, I could see why he’d stuck with it. With open source code, I could add silly but useful features, such as highlighting “TK”—a common shorthand in journalism for placeholder text—in a conspicuous color so that it was easy to locate later.

If nothing else, I came to appreciate that programming was a reliable way to enter that elusive mode called flow, a state in which we’re somehow forgetful and alert at once.

In the industry, there’s a general impression that fussing over programming languages is juvenile. If a job candidate expresses enthusiasm for languages, it could very well be a red flag. Being a “polyglot” is no virtue if you can read a menu in five languages but can’t write decent prose in one. And per the popular and influential essay “Choose Boring Technology,” using fancy, novel languages is at odds with serious, robust engineering—akin to how avant-garde poetics are unfit for drafting legal contracts.

Unfortunately for my employers, I had precisely zero fucks to give about serious engineering. I did, however, give a great many fucks about having a pleasurable time at my day job until I received the green card. I took on projects if they’d give me an opportunity to use a new language, which is a dumb reason, and for the same reason even volunteered to change teams, usually a bad career decision. At the obligatory company hackathon weeks, I hacked together domain-specific languages—those specialized for a narrow purpose. I made a meager public contribution by submitting a couple of low-hanging patches to the official TypeScript compiler. I picked up a few gold badges on Stack Overflow. The years went by and, what do you know, I became a pretty decent programmer.

Then everything changed, as it did for everyone else, in November 2022. When ChatGPT arrived, it was a wild card whose impact on the industry was far from obvious. Using neural networks to write code wasn’t new. Early readers of Andrej Karpathy’s blog will remember the 2015 post featuring plausible-looking C code generated by an LSTM—a precursor of the transformer—which nonetheless amounted to little more than a parlor trick.

I remember tasking GPT-4 with rewriting a simple JavaScript program in different languages. It performed like Google Translate, instantly and passably. I likened ChatGPT to the monkey’s paw from the eponymous horror story, the accursed object that grants your wishes but not without casualties. In dutiful pursuit of the given objective—and only that objective—LLMs produced a locally correct solution while mangling essential code around it.

Then, in early 2025, AI command-line tools were released. At first, what I saw wasn’t so much a leap in quality as a greater eagerness to volunteer help. Then they improved—at this point I should quote Hemingway’s line about how one goes bankrupt—gradually, then suddenly. Within a year, they became massively, belittlingly better at my job.

Some engineers still labor a great deal to remain unimpressed, quibbling about how “it’s good at X but not yet at Y.” Please. The flaws are there, sure, and guardrails are essential. But a better heuristic for their usefulness would be: If you had to choose, for the rest of your life, between pair-programming with a competent colleague and these tools, which would you want? Any programmer worth their salt must acknowledge that they won’t be as useful to their colleague as these tools are. Even if the colleague were Linus Torvalds, I would choose the LLMs, since they won’t give me hell for asking dumb questions.

For someone who feels no allegiance to tech, the usefulness of these coding tools triggers contradictory emotions. I find ChatGPT’s indecorous entry into the literary world intolerable, and I remember feeling a creeping nihilism about the writing profession when it was released. I know firsthand what a reliance on AI can do. As I’ve come to rely more on coding tools to hand me solutions, my ability to code has gone through a swift and decisive decline. Depend on it to write sentences and it will, like a neurodegenerative disease, blunt the finer senses that writers must protect the way pianists protect their hands.

Earlier this year, it was reported that more than 90 percent of code at companies like Anthropic and Coinbase is written with AI. As most people are prompting rather than hand-coding, much of modern software engineering has become machine-operated, not unlike Mitch McConnell these days.

While this may not be the end of programming, it’ll be the end of programmers. To keep calling those in this profession “programmers” would be incorrect, since the entity that does the programming is a platoon of agents, with humans at desks prompting something out of them. A more flattering term might be meta-programming, but it’d be more accurate to say that programmers have become operators of sorts.

An operator is someone a degree removed from the thing they manipulate. (Unlike someone chipping at rock with a pickaxe, an operator of mining equipment never touches it.) Perhaps one way to describe how the nature of the profession has changed is that it’s as if cooks had stopped making food themselves and taken to writing recipes instead. If you’ve spent any time around AI Twitter, you might be forgiven for thinking that software engineering is now about looking impressed by markdown files.

This isn’t necessarily something to be defeatist about, and I’m not pessimistic about the quality of engineering. When coding agents were first released, I wasn’t sure whether this was just another natural progression, since the story of programming is a story of ever higher levels of abstraction. From bytes to assembly to higher-level languages like Python, we’ve built tools to domesticate the complexity. And with AI, we’ve finally handed off tasks that are constitutionally unfit for the human brain.

But as programmers cede control over the final unit of manipulation, programming no longer qualifies as a craft. Hence my disagreement with Simon Willison, otherwise a voice of reason among witless clout chasers in AI discourse, who wrote, “Quitting programming as a career right now because of LLMs would be like quitting carpentry as a career thanks to the invention of the table saw.” With all due respect, this is a flawed analogy, as carpenters working with a table saw are still craftspeople.

This means there won’t be great individual programmers in the way that someone like Brian W. Kernighan was: the programmer laureate who cowrote what is possibly the greatest book on programming, The C Programming Language, by which we measure programming languages to this day. (It is the book that popularized the “Hello, World” convention.)

Another loss is that programming no longer induces the flow state—or it induces a shallow sort of flow at best. There’s still a certain “hookedness” to the activity, but whereas tackling a gnarly bug once meant begging every individual neuron in my brain to do its part, the work has become suspiciously effortless. That hookedness may be closer to what the cultural anthropologist Natasha Dow Schüll called, in her 2012 book Addiction by Design, the “machine zone,” the trancelike state of the Las Vegas gambler.

Even though I’ve stopped coding myself, I find I’ve been producing more code than ever before. The features I’d built for my open source editor in the past were unserious enough that I could bang out a quick program on a weekend afternoon or two. But with Claude Code, I’ve become addicted to making what has essentially become a heavyweight word processor, a juiced-up Scrivener. (My changelog tells me I’ve built 239 features and 17 extensions, mapped to 141 keyboard shortcuts.)

One Sunday this summer, I was disconcerted to discover that I’d spent 10 hours in Claude Code. How do I know? I used Claude Code to build a time-tracker for Claude Code usage. I resolved to cut back. I spent eight hours the next day. This compelled me to write a program, using Claude Code, to block myself from Claude Code except for two days a week, which I of course tried to undo with Claude Code, only to be valiantly defended by Claude Code.

With this borrowed competence has come a total loss of interest in learning new languages. A couple of years ago, an iOS meditation timer app I’d used for years started nudging me with ads, and I resolved to one day learn Swift, the language Apple created for iPhone and Mac apps, and build a simple version myself. But I now know that’s never going to happen, because I’ve already built it knowing zilch about Swift syntax, along with 12 more iOS and Mac apps: The machine helped me fork the long-unmaintained SelfControl into a more granular internet blocker; by reverse-engineering a clipboard manager—I was never sure it wasn’t leaking my input—it built a new one with an auditable security profile.

I suspect, going forward, that I’ll still mess around with code, but no longer as a professional. Recently, it became clear that I needed to quit my day job as a programmer to finish a book I’d set out to write. I once told a writer friend that I’d try to be a mole in this industry as long as I was in it. I did get to see some things while I stuck it out and, along the way, coaxed that green card from my employer.

I’m not too sorry to leave this profession at this moment of nuttiness. But I’ll admit that, after all those languages I enlisted to make my detour more tolerable, I came to appreciate the pleasures of polyglot programming. As humans cede control to the machine, it’ll be a joy lost to us all.

This is the final installment in a three-part Machine Readable series on AI-enabling languages. It may also be the final Machine Readable column, period.
