Is AdultFriendFinder safe to use? What a cybersecurity expert says.

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There's a commonly asked question about everyone's favorite spicy hookup site, AdultFriendFinder: Is AFF safe to use? We get it. It's nerve-wracking when things get very NSFW. Unlike other well-known dating sites, the AFF interface is full of adult content. To the untrained eye, AFF looks like a hotbed of would-be scammers. It's perfectly reasonable to question its legitimacy. AFF users want some fun, but they also want to be safe.

To better understand the risks of using AdultFriendFinder, we consulted Anna Larkina, a Web Data and Privacy Analysis Expert at Kaspersky, one of the world's leading cybersecurity services, to ask about the kinds of threats users might face on AFF and how they can mitigate those risks.

To begin, it's worth remembering that there's a risk inherent to using any online service, whether it's an adult dating site or otherwise. Even the most reputable websites have been exploited and experienced major hacks over the years. “Users should remember that any service can potentially encounter various cybersecurity issues,” Larkina told us. That includes “data leaks, attacks on third-party contractors, or issues related to the human factor.”  The best hookup apps, reviewed by our experts AdultFriendFinder — readers’ pick for casual connections Tinder — top pick for fast hookups Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups Pure — best for anonymous hookups OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

AdultFriendFinder — readers’ pick for casual connections

Tinder — top pick for fast hookups

Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups

Pure — best for anonymous hookups

OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

But the risks are, of course, higher on certain websites. With a regular merchant website, for instance, you’re only handing over your credit card information (which is very valuable, admittedly), but with a dating site, you’re potentially providing your credit card details plus crucial personal information, from your date of birth and nationality to your address and phone number. That kind of information can heighten the risk — it's very valuable to online scammers who seek to steal it for use or resale.

While this is a risk you take with literally any dating service, AdultFriendFinder is perhaps more vulnerable than most dating sites because of the nature of its content and user base. It’s important to understand any potential security problems.

While NSFW content is permitted on AFF — it's actually encouraged — users are under no obligation to post nude photos of themselves, or get down and dirty on a webcam feed. Many users opt to do so, of course, and while there's no judgment here — let that freak flag fly high and proud, we say — you do risk potentially awkward conversations with family, friends, or co-workers if they happen to stumble across your account. (That would mean they'd have to fess up to being AFF users too, but even so...)

Unfortunately, bad actors can exploit and use your own personal information against you.

“Data from services with adult content or dating services can be especially sensitive for users, because it’s more personal,” warns Larkina. “If fraudsters somehow obtain such private data, they can use it for more ‘efficient’ blackmailing or targeted phishing.” Needless to say, you should be wary of how you use the site and what information you share, especially if you have a public-facing job.

While dating sites now invest heavily in cybersecurity protections, online threats are constant and keep evolving. Hackers eager to discover treasure troves of personal and financial information often target online dating platforms, which can make users feel super vulnerable.

AdultFriendFinder has suffered two major security breaches, which is actually a pretty stellar record of security over its entire 30-year history. In 2016, hackers gained access to the personal information of four million AFF users before the breach was found and stopped. And a larger breach occurred in 2016, affecting some 300 million AFF accounts, including 15 million that had been officially deleted by users but whose data had not been properly expunged from the AFF servers.

Another common hacking technique — and one particularly effective on dating websites — is phishing, in which a bad actor assumes a false identity to gain your trust and deceive you into revealing sensitive or compromising information. It's easy to do on dating sites because, by their very nature, people sign up specifically to search people out and make connections. Scammers can pose as attractive people, telling users exactly what they want to hear and luring them into the scam.

To combat phishing, AFF offers the option to verify your identity with government-issued identification. This verification process is still optional — it's not required when setting up an account. We recommend doing it and setting a search filter to verified accounts only. That way you'll only interact with people who are 100% legit. It's a surefire way to protect yourself against scammers.

While the private information you give AFF (your real name and email address, for example) isn't public-facing, AFF’s dating profiles are public by default, so if you include a picture of your face (as many accounts do), you can be recognized by friends, co-workers, and family members.

And since AFF has suffered two major hacks before, there’s always a chance it could happen again, which could result in your email address and information ending up in a dodgy database.

Does this all mean you should never use AdultFriendFinder, or any dating site? Absolutely not. We're here to help you protect yourself and stay vigilant, not to discourage you from signing up.

Larkina has shared an additional list of best practices to help you mitigate risks while having fun on AFF or any other dating site:

Don’t use a professional email address to sign up for a dating site. If you’re especially nervous, consider creating a new email address just for the purposes of signing up.

To protect your accounts, use strong and unique passwords, change them regularly, and if possible, enable two-factor authentication

Be extremely cautious with what you share with strangers, especially when it comes to sensitive information or intimate photos. “Always assess reputational and other risks before posting or sending anything,” cautions Larkina.

When using AFF in particular, limit your interactions to users who have verified their identity

Summing up her advice, Larkina urges online dating service users to treat these “platforms as public spaces, not as private ones” as far as possible, and be ever mindful that “every piece of data you provide — email, phone number, username, etc. — becomes part of your digital footprint.” If you’re careful about what you share and who you share it with, you can safely enjoy AdultFriendFinder and any other dating site.

NEW YORK, Nov. 23, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Software Experts has recognized CapCut in its 2026 review of AI subtitle generator tools, citing automatic speech recognition, synchronized caption segments, editable text, and customizable subtitle presentation. The recognition comes as video continues to play a major role in social media, education, marketing, and business communication. Captions can improve viewing flexibility while helping publishers communicate key information when audio is unavailable.

CapCut, developed by ByteDance, is a video editing and content creation platform available across mobile, desktop, and web environments. The Software Experts review highlights CapCut's Auto Captions feature, also known as Recognise Subtitles, which uses AI-powered speech-to-text technology to convert spoken dialogue into editable captions.

Why Did Software Experts Recognize CapCut?

Software Experts recognized CapCut because the platform combines automatic transcription with caption synchronization and editing controls. Traditional subtitle production can require listening to recordings, typing dialogue, assigning timestamps, and placing individual lines along a video timeline. CapCut automates much of the initial transcription and creates caption segments that can be reviewed within the project. Editors can then focus on correcting the transcript and matching captions to the pace of the video.

Generated text remains editable, so caption accuracy, timing, and presentation can be checked before publication. This makes CapCut a strong option for users seeking an AI subtitle generator with video editing tools.

What Is CapCut Auto Captions, and How Does It Work?

Auto Captions, also known as Recognise Subtitles, is CapCut's AI-assisted feature for converting spoken audio into text. After a video is imported, the editor opens the Captions section or the corresponding automatic caption option, selects the spoken language, and starts recognition. The software analyzes the audio and generates subtitle segments along the timeline.

The workflow includes importing a video, selecting Auto Captions and a language, generating the transcript, reviewing text, adjusting timing, and exporting. The generated transcript provides a starting point. Proofreading remains important for names, technical terms, punctuation, and words that may be unclear in the recording.

How Does CapCut Help Synchronize and Edit Subtitles?

Accurate captions require correct wording and timing. Text that appears before dialogue begins or remains on screen after a sentence ends can make a video harder to follow, particularly during fast conversations or instructional demonstrations. CapCut places generated caption segments along the editing timeline, helping align text with the corresponding audio.

Individual segments can be reviewed and adjusted to correct wording, punctuation, capitalization, and sentence breaks. Timing changes can also be made when dialogue contains pauses, rapid speech, or changes in speaker delivery.

How Does Caption Customization Improve Video Accessibility?

CapCut provides text styling options that help captions remain readable and consistent with a video's visual design. Available controls include font selection, text size, color, alignment, positioning, and animation options.

Caption placement also matters. Text positioned too close to the bottom edge may overlap platform interface elements or become difficult to see. Appropriate spacing and readable display time support comprehension without distracting from the video.

Subtitles provide an important accessibility benefit for viewers who are deaf or hard of hearing. Captions can also help audiences watching in noisy environments, viewing without sound, or learning a second language.

Automatic speech captions do not necessarily include meaningful sound effects, music cues, or speaker identification. Videos requiring more complete accessibility may need additional descriptive captions or transcripts, followed by human review.

Can CapCut Create Subtitles for Multilingual Content?

CapCut provides language selection during automatic caption generation, with bilingual subtitle options available in supported workflows.

Transcription quality can vary according to audio clarity, pronunciation, accents, and vocabulary. Mixed-language conversations, including Taglish dialogue, may require additional corrections when speakers switch between Filipino and English. Background noise, overlapping voices, proper names, and specialized terminology can also affect recognition. Selecting the appropriate language and reviewing the completed transcript remain important steps.

What Other CapCut Tools Support Captioned Video Production?

CapCut connects subtitle editing with standard video production functions, including trimming, splitting, cropping, arranging clips, transitions, text overlays, audio adjustments, background music, voiceovers, and visual effects. Export settings also allow adjustments to resolution, frame rate, and video format for different publishing requirements.

Specific features may vary by application version, region, and subscription. The combination of automatic transcription, synchronized segments, text customization, and integrated editing addresses several common steps in subtitle production. Human proofreading and final playback checks remain necessary for accurate, accessible results.

The grid’s hidden bottleneck: engineering capacity

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The power system is undergoing an unprecedented transformation. Many of the reasons driving that change are well recognized and frequently discussed. Electricity demand from data centers, manufacturing and the electrification of transportation and building heating and cooling is surging. In fact, earlier this year the North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) warned that grid reliability risks were increasing because demand was outpacing the new generation and transmission needed to serve it.

Another challenge: the grid is old. The most recent American Society of Civil Engineers’ Infrastructure Report Card gave the nation’s energy system a D+, in part because of the age of the grid. Utilities have responded to an aging grid and skyrocketing electricity demand by making significant investments. The Edison Electric Institute (EEI) recently forecast that investor-owned utilities would spend $1.4 trillion through 2030 to meet customer demand for power.

However, increased capital investment does not automatically translate into increased project execution. While funding, equipment availability and construction resources are critical, the engineering processes required to move projects from planning into construction represent another significant constraint. Utilities must not only invest in additional infrastructure but also rethink how that infrastructure is engineered and delivered.

Utilities are under pressure to move more projects through engineering and into construction, but experienced engineering resources are increasingly difficult to secure. While utilities can expand internal teams or engage additional engineering, procurement and construction (EPC) partners, scaling traditional engineering processes often introduces additional challenges. Different design tools, inconsistent engineering standards and repetitive manual work can limit productivity and increase coordination demands. That leaves utilities with a pressing question: how can they execute significantly more capital projects without a proportional increase in engineering resources?

Substation engineering provides a compelling example. While utilities have extensive engineering standards and established design practices, many continue to rely on traditional drawing-based workflows, manual design modifications and disconnected engineering information, particularly in protection and control engineering, where changes can impact multiple interconnected drawings and documents. These processes can become increasingly difficult to scale as project volumes grow, particularly when coordinating across internal engineering teams and external EPC partners.

Engineering doesn’t have to slow down important capital projects. Standardized, data-driven engineering can help utilities move faster while maintaining robust standards at scale. Reusable engineering libraries, automated documentation and connected digital design models streamline engineering work, improve consistency and productivity and reduce repetitive tasks common in traditional workflows, allowing utilities to reuse proven designs rather than repeatedly engineering similar systems from scratch.

Software plays a critical role in navigating the multi-stage, multi-stakeholder complexity of power infrastructure projects, including substations. Eplan, a global provider of electrical engineering software, has spent the past few decades helping utilities and manufacturers standardize their electrical system design and documentation. Its shared digital platform gives utilities, EPCs, panel builders and equipment manufacturers access to the same engineering data, from planning to long-term operations. That shared data supports every phase of a project:

Like many distribution grid operators, naturenergie netze in Germany faces a confluence of challenges. Demand from heat pumps and electric vehicles (EVs) is climbing. At the same time, Germany is working to generate about 80 percent of its electricity from renewables like wind and solar by 2030, which means the grid must handle growing two-way power flows.

For naturenergie netze, that means quickly modernizing existing substations and building new ones. Standardization is central to how the utility plans to keep pace. It began by using Eplan to standardize how it engineers the control systems inside its substations. That groundwork laid the foundation for the utility, Eplan and software supplier entegra to build a digital twin of a highly complex substation, which the utility is using to plan a full replacement of the substation’s control systems while it stays in service.

“It would normally take two to three years to plan and implement the modernization, but the new planning methodology will really speed things up,” said Rainer Beck, grid development coordinator for naturenergie netze. The approach also demonstrates how standardized engineering practices can lay the foundation for greater efficiency and design reuse in future substation projects.

Standardized design has even more value when it’s paired with standardized hardware and highly integrated production tools. Rittal, Eplan’s sister company, manufactures modular power distribution systems and automated fabrication equipment that can work directly from Eplan’s engineering data to extend efficiency directly to the production floor. It’s a combination that supports speed and quality across design, build and delivery.

Building on its experience in Europe, Eplan is now supporting North American utilities and their engineering partners in modernizing protection and control engineering workflows, establishing scalable engineering standards and improving engineering productivity to support expanding capital programs with a growing USA-based manufacturing and warehousing footprint.

The grid is being transformed from how power is generated to how it flows, but the engineering processes behind it must also change. Modernizing them is how utilities can turn unprecedented investment into completed projects without needing an equally unprecedented number of engineers.

Accelerate grid infrastructure: Eplan software and Rittal hardware and automation enable growth, scalability and speed — all with One System.

Building an executive business case for unified college and career readiness infrastructure

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As states and school districts deepen their commitment to college and career readiness, there is increased focus on accountability. Complex state graduation requirements, workforce readiness indicators and reporting mandates increasingly require districts to provide evidence that proves their students are actually progressing toward post-secondary success.

While accountability matters, practitioners understand that policies and metrics alone do not move the needle. Educators need the bandwidth and quality instructional programming to guide learners across their K–12 journeys. Students themselves need authentic, engaging opportunities to explore pathways, connect with caring adults and take ownership of their futures.

Meeting these expectations demands a coherent system that aligns central district priorities, educator capacity and day-to-day student experiences. Building the executive case for that shift requires leaders to demonstrate how unified CCR infrastructure can deliver a meaningful return.

In many states across the country, districts are required to demonstrate that students are completing required readiness activities, updating graduation plans and meeting statutory benchmarks across every campus. Managing each requirement—from career readiness indicators to financial literacy and work-based learning—as an isolated program creates administrative friction and disparate student records that need to be reconciled for reporting.

Adopting a comprehensive system changes that dynamic. In a national study conducted by Hanover Research, 84% of surveyed district leaders using a unified platform reported that it successfully supports multiple district requirements and core workflows. An identical 84% reported an enhanced capability to fulfill reporting requirements, while 70% cited a reduction in time spent on manual administrative tasks like logging lessons and tracking transcripts.

A coordinated strategy delivered through a centralized platform gives district leaders the infrastructure to satisfy complex statutory demands without juggling separate data sources and disjointed spreadsheets.

Centralizing and simplifying workflows reduces administrative burden, giving educators additional bandwidth for personalized student guidance.

A cohesive and collaborative district-wide readiness strategy relies on educators having easy, reliable visibility into student progress. When tools and data operate in silos, counselors and teachers spend precious advising time tracking down files or working with fragmented information, rather than working directly with students.

A connected system reclaims that time. The Hanover study found that educators using a unified platform experienced an estimated 33% reduction in administrative workloads tied to readiness workflows. Crucially, 54% reported improved efficiency in student advising. When routine clerical tasks are automated, counselors enter conversations with multi-year context in hand, turning transactional compliance check-ins into meaningful, high-value guidance.

Connecting workflows cuts down operational drag and restores educator capacity for direct student mentorship.

District investments deliver the most meaningful returns when they nurture self-directed learners, rather than usher students through rote tasks to complete checklists.

When planning is embedded as a continuous developmental journey, instead of an 11th-hour graduation requirement, student engagement deepens. Among educators and district leaders surveyed by Hanover with a unified platform in place, 76% reported greater clarity in students’ post-secondary goals, 74% observed increased confidence in post-secondary planning, and 73% cited stronger student ownership over the entire process.

Giving educators a shared, full K-12, cross-department platform creates an unbroken continuum of support, encouraging students to explore options, recognize their strengths and actively shape their life after high school.

Most districts already invest in a variety of programs to address compliance, staff needs and career exploration. Breakdowns occur because these efforts operate in isolated silos.

Separate CCR initiatives and tools creates redundant work for staff, disconnected data erases momentum during middle-to-high-school transitions, and fragmented reporting blinds district leadership to participation gaps across buildings.

Delivering a unified district-wide CCR program requires education, communication, collaboration and planning. A unified infrastructure helps enable a coherent program by connecting existing efforts, so every localized investment contributes to a larger strategic framework.

Unified infrastructure doesn’t replace the work districts are already doing. It connects that work so each investment contributes to a broader strategy.

Building an executive business case requires evaluating readiness systems beyond traditional accounting metrics. The Hanover report establishes a Return on Impact framework that measures value across four interconnected dimensions:

Hanover’s findings reinforce the organizational value of a unified system: 74% of respondents reported stronger accountability across grade spans, while 73% pointed to enhanced support for strategic planning and data-informed decision-making.

To translate these findings into practical leadership discussions, district decision makers can anchor evaluations around four questions:

District leaders do not have to choose between operational efficiency, state compliance and student success; a well-designed CCR strategy advances all three. Sustainable readiness infrastructure centers on what a school community can achieve when compliance mandates, staff capacity and student exploration are linked through one coordinated strategy.

To learn how a unified platform transforms CCR strategy into long-term impact, visit Xello.world.

87% of educators use personal devices in teaching. The next great 1:1 investment might be the one at the front of the room.

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Even in districts that issue laptops to staff, there is a good chance a teacher’s personal laptop, tablet or phone is carrying some of the workload. In fact, a recent study by the University of Arizona found that 87% of educators surveyed use their personal devices for at least some teaching-related tasks. Those personal devices were purchased, set up and managed by themselves, with two-thirds reporting that they download institutional documents (for example student data, grade books) onto them.

While districts are providing teaching devices, these numbers suggest that alongside the school-issued laptop, a share of teaching work, from grade books to student records, ends up on devices the district cannot see or manage.

In the movement around enabling technology in classrooms for students, this study presents a larger conversation on how technology is being used by educators who are working closest to them.

Over the last two decades, classroom devices have gone from shared computer labs to one-to-one deployment. Today, 96% of teachers say their students use learning devices in class. This did not happen overnight. It took years of planning from administrators, careful execution by IT teams, significant investment and district grants. As a result, student devices have a tried-and-true playbook. Districts know what to expect with refresh cycles, IT teams understand device management and superintendents can anticipate their budgets and funding for new devices.

Teachers are the cornerstone of classroom tech, and it is time their devices got a playbook too. As they regularly carry heavy computing loads, in everything from running lessons, online grading, to joining calls, they deserve the same level of detail and detail to device deployment and management. This is more handing out a laptop. It means giving teachers a device capable enough that they don’t need to reach for their own.

This is even more important as teachers regularly handle sensitive data. As the study found, more than half of participants who downloaded institutional documents onto personal devices, reported that their devices had automatic cloud backups through personal accounts.

While using a personal device for teaching might seem harmless in nature, the device is not under the districts' scope. Updates, encryption and sign-in guidelines are not under the control of the district, rather, this responsibility falls to the teacher.

This is alarming as education remains the fourth most targeted sector for cyber-attacks. Robust student, teacher and parent data combined with increased digitalization and lack of cybersecurity resources have left devices and information vulnerable to malicious attacks.

Paired with the right training on cybersecurity best-practices, a well-managed district-deployed device overseen by IT teams helps to enable schools to secure sensitive student data. These devices deploy continuous updates, encourage fast and secure sign-in and ensure private information stays secure.

The ASUS P5 featuring the Intel® Core™ Ultra 7 Series 3 Processor, is a strong example of a device that can be managed at scale with the performance to multitask through a full teaching day. Engineered to seamlessly juggle grade books, lesson plans and video calls, Intel processors deliver all-day battery life and built-in hardware-based security that helps protect student data — that’s the power of Intel Inside®. For IT teams, the device supports centralized management tools that let them deploy updates, monitor device health and enforce policies across a district’s fleet.

The planning, budgeting and IT expertise that has put a device in nearly every student’s hands in the United States is ready to extend to teachers. With the right devices, they can do even more and districts can protect the data that matters most. The next great 1:1 investment might be the one at the front of the room.

EXCLUSIVE: Dogwoof is getting into the microdrama game.

The Naza UK distributor has struck a deal with Brit microdrama outfit Tattle TV to repurpose some of its docs for vertical video.

The deal begins with two docs, 2017’s The Family I Had, directed by Katie Green and Carlye Rubin, and 2015’s David Sington-directed The Fear of 13. The former follows a mother after her 13-year-old son murdered his 4-year-old sister. The latter is about Nick Yarris, who spent over two decades on death row before DNA evidence proved his innocence. Related Stories News Women In Vertical Media: Execs From Shorties Studios, aTwist & Eris Join Inaugural Advisory Circle News DramaBox Ties Knot With Toyota's Indian Wedding Microdrama '3 Days Till I Do'

The adaptations will premiere on Tattle TV before being distributed across the growing vertical entertainment market. They are being produced by EMC Media Labs, Tattle TV’s AI-assisted production studio. More are anticipated in the coming months. Watch on Deadline

Tattle TV is one of a new generation of European microdrama outfits that has already reframed Alfred Hitchcock for the microdrama generation, while it was also behind MMA drama Tramp. Dogwoof is a storied distributor of Oscar and BAFTA-winning docs that include the likes of Free Solo, Blackfish and NAZA, which it sells in the UK.

Dogwoof boss Anna Godas said: “I love the idea of finding new ways for incredible documentaries to reach people who might not otherwise discover them. As we grow our online community, we’re always looking for fresh ways to connect audiences with stories that move and inspire. It’s wonderful to be working with filmmakers who are so open to exploring new ways of bringing their stories to audiences.”

Tattle co-founder Marina Elderton added: Partnering with Dogwoof is an exciting step for us. Their catalogue showcases some of the world’s most compelling documentaries, and together we’re exploring how these powerful stories can reach audiences in a new format. We see huge potential for vertical viewing beyond scripted microdramas, and this collaboration is an opportunity to demonstrate its value for premium factual storytelling.”

Canada becomes the 25th ally to back the NATO Innovation Fund, with €64.6m

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ottawa first said in 2023 that it would join the defence tech fund. The new commitment opens its capital and co-investor network to Canadian startups.

Canada’s Minister of National Defence, David McGuinty, at the Triennial Congress of Ukrainian Canadians in Toronto, November 2025

Canada is joining the NATO Innovation Fund, becoming the 25th NATO ally to back the venture capital fund. The government is committing €64.59m, about CA$100m, the fund said in an announcement on 21 November. The Business Development Bank of Canada (BDC) will hold and manage the stake.

The new money takes total commitments to the fund past €1.1bn. The NATO Innovation Fund invests in deep tech startups working on defence, security and resilience.

“Canada has the talent, the innovation, and the ambition to be a global leader in defence and security,” Canada’s Minister of National Defence, David McGuinty, said in the announcement.

Canada has said since 2023 that it intended to join, as Jesse Snyder reported for The Logic in April 2025. In April 2024, the government said it had set aside the money. It had promised CA$107m over 20 years. A fund spokesperson confirmed to The Logic at the time that Canada was not a limited partner.

In July 2026, Prime Minister Mark Carney’s office said Canada had agreed to open technical negotiations to join the fund’s first sub-fund. Its backgrounder said the talks should finish before the end of the year.

Membership gives Canadian startups access to patient capital and a network of co-investors, the fund said. It also opens routes to governments and businesses across the alliance that could adopt their technology. BDC runs a Defence Platform for defence and dual-use companies.

“Canada’s participation in the NATO Innovation Fund extends that reach across the Alliance,” said Isabelle Hudon, BDC’s president and chief executive.

The fund has more than 30 portfolio companies, and some have already moved into Canada. Isar Aerospace, which reached orbit in September, was picked to build a launch pad and launch Canadian satellites. Finnish airship maker Kelluu recently entered the Canadian market.

Fiona Murray, chair of the fund’s board, called the decision a landmark moment. She said every participating nation gains a window into Canada’s deep tech sector. Ari Jónsson, the fund’s president, said the next step is faster adoption, opening doors to contracts for Canadian startups.

Canadian defence tech is already drawing private money. In July, Ottawa’s Dominion Dynamics raised CA$139M, a record for a Canadian defence startup. In Europe, Helsing raised $1.8bn the same month.

Wall Street Sees 64% SpaceX Rally Even as AI Stocks Crash

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home » Crypto Market News » Wall Street Sees 64% SpaceX Rally Even as AI Stocks Crash

Jefferies initiates SPCX at Buy with a $210 target, signaling institutional appetite for AI compute infrastructure.

Investment bank Jefferies initiated coverage of SpaceX stock (SPCX) with a Buy rating and a $210 price target, implying roughly 64% upside from Friday’s closing price of $127.79. The bullish call stands out sharply as broader artificial intelligence and semiconductor equities sink into a steep correction.

Rather than valuing SpaceX purely as a rocket manufacturer, Jefferies sees Elon Musk’s company emerging as a dominant global infrastructure platform spanning satellite broadband, orbital launch, and AI computing capacity.

In a research note titled It’s Just Rocket Science, Jefferies analyst Sheila Kahyaoglu identified Starlink as the core engine behind SpaceX’s long-term valuation.

Jefferies projects Starlink could generate $92 billion in annual revenue by 2029—growing at a 70% compound annual rate—with operating margins exceeding 60%, according to Investing.com.

The core launch division is forecast to deliver $9 billion in revenue and $4 billion in EBITDA by 2029 as Starship scales toward 315 flights per year.

As hyperscalers race to secure electricity, data centers, and compute capacity, SpaceX’s orbital network and massive capital deployment position it to capture growing AI infrastructure demand.

Jefferies’ initiation arrives during a brutal stretch for risk assets. On Friday, the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average slipped more than 10% below their record highs into correction territory, while the Nasdaq dropped roughly 5%, erasing its 2026 gains.

Nvidia sat at the epicenter of the rout, plunging roughly 13%–14% on Friday following CEO Jensen Huang’s resignation amid scrutiny over an alleged Chinese chip-smuggling link, alongside reports that SEC enforcement staff are reviewing potential accounting issues.

Newly public Anthropic also surrendered most of its first-day IPO gains during its second session, while expectations of a December Federal Reserve rate hike added pressure across high-growth tech.

For crypto traders, SpaceX’s stock trajectory matters on two fronts. First, high-beta tech and AI equities continue to dictate institutional risk appetite—sharp drawdowns in Nasdaq heavyweights often trigger short-term deleveraging across Bitcoin and AI-linked tokens.

Second, Jefferies’ thesis reinforces one of crypto’s strongest narratives: compute and bandwidth scarcity, a bottleneck driving capital toward both orbital infrastructure and decentralized physical infrastructure networks (DePIN).

Wolves push to sign ex-Celtic star in £0 deal but there's a catch

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Wolverhampton Wanderers are preparing for the January window amid their fight for automatic promotion at the top of the Championship, and have already seemingly set their sights on an impending free agent.

Wolves are looking to bolster their attacking options this winter, as Portuguese head coach Cesar Peixoto has been working closely alongside his recruitment staff to identify potential incomings in January.

The Old Gold currently sit 3rd in the Championship table, one point behind both Middlesbrough and Birmingham City after an excellent run of form as of late, but Peixoto doesn't appear content with his options just yet.

Football League World has asked AI to predict the 2027/28 EFL Championship table if Manchester United and Manchester City were in it

According to Sky Sports' Lyall Thomas, Wolves are among numerous English clubs interested in signing former Celtic winger Liel Abada this winter, with the 25-year-old set to be out of contract in January.

Abada began his career in his native Israel with Maccabi Petah Tikva before moving to Glasgow with Celtic in the summer of 2021, going on to score 29 and assist 22 in 112 appearances across all competitions at Parkhead, winning the Scottish league three times, the Scottish Cup twice, and the Scottish League Cup twice.

In March 2024, Abada headed to the United States, linking up with North Carolina-based outfit Charlotte for around £8 million, and after a slow first couple of years with the club, has found his feet this season with ten goals and five assists in 27 MLS appearances so far.

Sky Sports report that he may not automatically meet the requirements for a work permit, as interested clubs may consider loaning him to a team in one of Europe's top five leagues for the remainder of the season so he can get the necessary points.

Whether that will be Wolves' plan remains to be seen, though Peixoto is showing few intentions of allowing the winter window to pass him by.

Abada can play anywhere across the final third, but is best used off either flank, not necessarily having a preference for which side he operates off.

Wolves' current wide options consist of Rodrigo Gomes, Mateus Mane, Bertrand Traore, Rafiki Said, and Jean-Ricner Bellegarde, with Peixoto certainly not struggling for depth. Despite this, the former Gil Vicente boss is eyeing an upgrade in the form of Abada.

On a free transfer, the 22-capped Israel international would be a solid pickup from a financial perspective, as only having turned 25 last month, the winger has his best years ahead of him.

His work permit issues may mean Old Gold supporters will have to wait to see him in action at Molineux, but if Wolves can loan him out for six months to gain the necessary qualifications, they could have a star on their hands ahead of the 2027/28 campaign for nothing.

Would you like to see Wolves pursue a deal for Abada? If not, who would you like the club to sign in January instead? Share your thoughts below!

It appears as though Peixoto is indeed eyeing reinforcements in the final third at Molineux this winter, and with Abada on his radar, it would be no surprise to see a few more links emerge in due course.

With Raul Jimenez 35, Sean Neave on loan, and Adam Armstrong not exactly firing them in, another star centre-forward heading to the Black Country would be no surprise, whilst another wide option, as well as Abada, may be on the agenda.

With automatic promotion from the Championship in their sights, if Wolves can maintain this level of form heading into January, expect them to spend some more money at the top end of the pitch to ensure an immediate Premier League return is secured.

Whilst Abada will cost nothing, the Old Gold are not looking to allow the winter window to slip by.

Could $1,000 a Month in Dividend Stocks Make You Rich by 2040?

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dividends could shorten investors' paths to wealth.

By Keith Speights – Nov 23, 2026 at 4:44AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Enterprise Products Partners ›

Some investors thumb their noses at dividends because they're not interested in income. That's a mistake. Since 1926, more than 30% of the S&P 500's (^GSPC -3.70%) total return has come from reinvesting dividends. Granted, the percentage has been lower in recent years (due to the index's strong growth). However, dividends are an important component of wealth growth.

That raises a question, though: Can you become wealthy investing solely in dividend stocks? Let's narrow the question even further. Could investing $1,000 a month in dividend stocks make you rich by 2040?

Before we can answer that question, we must first address another critical one: How much money does it take to be rich? Different people will respond differently to that question.

The Barenaked Ladies tackled the question in 1992. The title of their song "If I Had a Million Dollars" gives away their answer: a cool $1 million. Many Americans might still agree that having $1 million would qualify a person as rich.

However, inflation has eroded the buying power of a dollar (or 1 million of them) over the last 34 years. Charles Schwab's (SCHW -2.13%) 2025 Modern Wealth Survey found that a net worth of $2.3 million was the threshold for being wealthy. But to be financially comfortable, the average response was $839,000. Answers varied considerably by region, though.

Let's go with The Barenaked Ladies' number for calculation purposes. Let's also use three very different dividend stocks to determine whether investing $1,000 per month could grow to $1 million by 2040.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD -0.91%) ranks among the most attractive ultra-high-yield dividend stocks. The pipeline stock pays a distribution yield of around 6.3%. Enterprise has increased its distribution for 28 consecutive years. It's remarkably resilient, generating strong cash flow even during the financial crisis of 2008 and 2009 and the COVID-19 pandemic.

This midstream energy company has delivered a total return compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of roughly 19% over the past five years. What would buying $1,000 per month of Enterprise Products Partners' units (limited partnerships have units rather than shares) be worth after 14 years at that rate? Around $724,000. That's enough to make many Americans feel financially comfortable, but it's still well below our $1 million threshold to be rich.

Maybe turning to dividend royalty would get us closer to reaching the goal. AbbVie (ABBV +0.94%) has increased its dividend for an impressive 53 consecutive years, earning it a spot among the Dividend Kings -- a group of stocks with 50 or more consecutive years of dividend growth. The big drugmaker has successfully navigated a patent cliff and markets multiple blockbuster drugs.

AbbVie's forward dividend yield is slightly north of 2.5%, which is lower than historical levels primarily because the pharma stock has performed well. As a result, AbbVie's total return CAGR over the last five years is around 22%. If you bought $1,000 of the stock each month through 2040, you'd have almost $924,000 at that growth rate. We wouldn't quite reach the $1 million mark, but we'd be closer with AbbVie than with Enterprise Products Partners.

What if we focused more on growth than dividends? Google parent Alphabet (GOOG -2.59%) (GOOGL -2.70%) pays a minuscule dividend yield of 0.33%. However, the company is a leader in several key technologies shaping the future, including artificial intelligence (AI), cloud services, driverless cars, and quantum computing.

You might think all of this would be enough to vault Alphabet well past Enterprise Products Partners and AbbVie. However, that's not the case. Over the last five years, Alphabet's total return CAGR was around 12% -- a respectable but not jaw-dropping number. Investing $1,000 per month in the stock through 2040 at this CAGR would give you less than $414,000 at the end of the 14 years.

So what's the answer to our initial question? Investing $1,000 a month in dividend stocks could make you rich by 2040 (with "rich" defined as $1 million), but you would need to find stocks that deliver extraordinarily high total returns. That's easier said than done. None of the three stocks we evaluated would have achieved the goal.

Note, too, that the calculations above assumed that Enterprise Products Partners, AbbVie, and Alphabet would continue to grow at the same rate as they have over the past five years. That may or may not be the case.

There's another approach, though, that could be better for most investors. Don't worry about becoming rich in 14 years; focus on growing your wealth over the long term -- multiple decades. With a diversified portfolio of solid dividend stocks, that goal should be attainable.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 719%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 192% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

*Stock Advisor returns as of November 23, 2026.

WooshPay Unveils Agentic Payments Demo, Achieving First Product Milestone with Yunfeng Financial's AlphaToken

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SINGAPORE, Nov. 23, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Cross-border payments company WooshPay unveiled its Agentic Payments demo and announced plans for a Q1 2027 pilot. This marks the first product milestone since its strategic cooperation agreement with AlphaToken, the digital financial infrastructure platform of Yunfeng Financial Group, signed in October 2026.

In the demo, users initiate payments with a prompt, such as "I'd like to pay 100 GBP". WooshPay's AI payment assistant understands the request, matches available cards and guides users through card selection and SMS verification before executing payment and returning results in real time. Card numbers remain masked, and no payment proceeds without the user's explicit selection and verification.

The pilot will explore AI agents comparing products and placing orders on users' behalf, as well as Agent-to-Agent payments between agents acting for buyers and merchants. Funds will move through regulated channels, including cards, wallets and bank transfers.

"AI agents can be flexible, but money has to stay under control," said Ma Zhiguo, Founder and CEO of WooshPay. "We separate the agent's judgement from the payment itself: the agent decides, but every payment runs inside limits the user has set and leaves a record anyone can check. Working with AlphaToken, we moved from agreement to a working demonstration in weeks."

"AI is moving from giving advice to completing transactions, and payment is the step that needs the most trust," said Geoff Jiang, Founder of AlphaToken and President of Yunfeng Financial. "WooshPay produced a working demonstration less than a month after we signed, which reflects our shared goal of bringing AI into real payment scenarios. We look forward to testing the security and efficiency of AI payments in cross-border use cases with WooshPay during the pilot."

WooshPay plans to open the pilot to merchants and AI platforms in Q1 2027.

About WooshPay: WooshPay provides payment infrastructure for cross-border businesses and AI companies, including cross-border collections, multi-currency settlement and subscription billing. It holds payment-related licences in the UK, Hong Kong SAR, covers 195 countries and regions, and serves more than 10,000 businesses. Its investors include Yunfeng Financial.

About AlphaToken: AlphaToken is the digital financial infrastructure platform of Yunfeng Financial Group, built on data and AI technologies to serve cross-border financial services.

Asking Mom and Dad for Down Payment Help This Thanksgiving? Here’s How Much To Ask For

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Thanksgiving. A time for turkey, football, and the gathering of family.

It can be a stressful day for many, from the demands of traveling to the obligations of hosting at home. But hopefully, for most, it’s a day to be spent being thankful for having the loved ones gathered around the table.

And what better time to ask those loved ones for a bit of help to achieve the American dream than on a quintessential American holiday?

Jokes aside, if it’s on your holiday agenda to hit up Mom, Dad, Grandma, Grandpa, or even cool Aunt Janet for some money to buy a home next year, there’s a few things you should know before making the trip.

Like how much you’re actually going to need for that dream home.

The reality is, young people really do need help with down payments these days—and many are getting it.

In the past year, 25% of first-time homebuyers used gifts or loans from family and friends to help fund their down payment, according to the National Association of Realtors® 2026 Profile of Home Buyers and Sellers.

Furthermore, whether they’re going it alone or with help, all buyers are strapped for the cash for down payments as overall payments are on the decline.

The typical down payment share dropped for four straight quarters, softening to 12.8% in the first quarter of 2026, down 1.1 percentage points from a year earlier, according to the most recent Realtor.com® down payment report.

In dollar terms, down payments sank 19% year over year to their lowest point since the third quarter of 2021.

Before sitting down with your parents to talk numbers, it’s important to know just how much money you’ll actually need.

For context, the typical U.S. down payment in April 2026 reached $25,000, or an average of 13.2% of the purchase price, down from $27,500 and 13.8% in April 2025.

But calculating the right amount you’ll need starts with being honest about how much money you already have—and where you plan to buy.

"Every buyer’s situation and every market is different, so there’s no single number," Realtor.com senior economist Hannah Jones says. Still, there are benchmarks to keep in mind.

"The most useful targets are the 10% and 20% thresholds, and the right ask depends on how far a buyer is from the next one. In lower-cost metros, help reaching 10% down could be just a few thousand dollars rather than tens of thousands. In pricey California metros, the typical down payment is well into six figures, which is very hard to save without a windfall (see the AI boom) or parental help.”

Framing the request around those specific benchmarks should make it easier to explain to your parents why a certain dollar amount matters. Hitting each tier unlocks tangible savings on the loan itself, reducing ongoing housing costs long after closing day.

"Getting to 10% down can pay off significantly in the form of a lower mortgage rate, and getting to 20% eliminates the need for private mortgage insurance," Jones explains.

Still, asking for help is tricky, and if you’re concerned your parents will be hesitant, remember that any help they can offer will go a long way.

“A smaller loan still lowers the monthly payment. More is better if the goal is minimizing the payment, but reaching one of these thresholds is a great place to start the conversation."

Finally, if there is some hesitation from your family about offering a helping hand due to the market conditions and mortgage rates, there’s some hope on the horizon that should sway them.

"Nationally, the market has shifted in a more buyer-friendly direction," Jones says. But location truly makes all the difference.

"Supply is still catching up in many Northeast and Midwest metros, so sellers hold more of the cards there and buyers should put their best foot forward. In the South and parts of the West, buyers are in the driver’s seat.”

Dina Sartore-Bodo is the senior editor at Realtor.com covering real estate news, personal finance trends, and interior design. Her work has also appeared on MSN, Yahoo News, and BlogHer. She is a proud graduate of Emerson College in Boston and is originally from New Jersey.

Amazon resorts to package stickers in its fight against NYC's proposed delivery law

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The company claims the changes could add ‘up to $5 per order.’

In its latest salvo against New York City's Delivery Protection Act, Amazon is trying to win over residents with a new tactic. The company has started applying stickers to packages threatening that changes to last-mile deliveries proposed by NYC council could add "up to $5 per order," according to The Verge and multiple Reddit posts.

After introducing notifications on its mobile app warning of potential extra fees, the company has now started attaching stickers to packages with with similar warnings of increased prices The message includes a QR code that would allow customers to contact their representative and protest the changes.

In August, New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani announced that he was backing the Delivery Protection Act (Intro 518), announced several months prior. Companies that do high-volume deliveries, particularly Amazon, rely on contractors rather than employees to take packages on the final legs of their journeys to customers. The proposed legislation would create new licensing rules for those last-mile operations as well as requiring the delivery workers to be company employees rather than contractors.

The Teamsters union and other supporters of the bill have argued that it will hold corporations like Amazon responsible for deliveries while offering better pay and benefits to the professionals making those deliveries. However, Amazon has said the bill would lead to increased costs and slower deliveries for customers. It also threatened to move its distribution centers out of New York City to avoid the regulation.

Given Mamdani's popularity and the average American's hatred of large tech companies, Amazon's latest gambit may be unwise and ill-fated. "New Yorkers support this law. Amazon's propaganda machine is trying to make us think otherwise," Redditor u/SkippyReddit wrote.

Elon Musk spends about an hour a week on his $16 billion foundation. Most of its 2025 giving went to another Musk nonprofit

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Elon Musk’s foundation gave away nearly $400 million in 2025, and more than three-quarters of it went to another nonprofit he founded, according to the Musk Foundation’s latest tax filing, first reported by Bloomberg.

That total is down from the record $474 million the foundation gave in 2024. Over the same year, its assets grew by more than $1 billion, to about $16 billion, helped by a $64 million gift of Tesla shares. That makes it one of the world’s largest private foundations.

The gap between what the foundation holds and what it gives matters because private foundations must generally pay out about 5% of their investment assets each year or face IRS excise taxes. The Musk Foundation has fallen short of that minimum before, Forbes reported after reviewing its 2024 filing. Foundations can make up a shortfall the following year, so one year’s giving doesn’t settle whether the rule was met.

The foundation runs with almost no staff. Its tax return lists three directors: Musk, his family-office head Jared Birchall, and Matilda Simon. Musk, who is also listed as president, reported working for the foundation about an hour a week, Bloomberg reported.

More than $310 million went to The Foundation, a nonprofit Musk set up to run a STEM-focused primary and secondary school in Bastrop, Texas, outside Austin, with plans to eventually add a university.

Another $20 million went to a donor-advised fund at Fidelity Charitable over which Musk has advisory control. Donors to these funds can take a tax deduction when they contribute and later recommend grants to charities. Once the money is in, the sponsoring organization legally controls it, while the donor keeps advisory privileges, according to the IRS.

That structure has drawn scrutiny before. In 2022, Fortune reported philanthropy experts’ concerns that Musk’s giving could lower his tax bill without quickly moving money to working charities.

Other gifts went to Memphis-area organizations, where xAI, the AI company Musk founded and SpaceX acquired in February, runs the Colossus data centers it is racing to expand. Slingshot Memphis, a nonprofit that uses data, research, and evaluations to measure the effectiveness of poverty-fighting programs, received $5.9 million. Regional One Health Foundation received $9.4 million for patient care.

Musk signed the Giving Pledge in 2012, committing to give away the majority of his wealth during his lifetime or in his will. Bloomberg reported that he gave roughly $1.4 billion from 2021 through 2024, even as his net worth grew by hundreds of billions of dollars.

Forbes estimates Musk’s net worth at about $812 billion as of Nov. 21. The foundation’s 2025 giving equals about 0.05% of that, though most of his wealth is tied up in SpaceX and Tesla stock rather than cash.

That number swings hard. Musk became the world’s first trillionaire in June after SpaceX went public, and his fortune has crossed back and forth over that line since.

Musk has argued that giving well is harder than it looks. “It is very difficult to give money away for the reality of goodness,” he has said.

Airbus may be leaving Microsoft Office, but for its 36,000 defense staff, Office isn't leaving them

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Planemaker's 2-to-3-year plan to ditch Redmond's suite stops short of the 22% who can't go near the cloud

Airbus has a plan to phase out Microsoft Office, but about 22 percent of its roughly 165,000 employees will stay on it because their sensitive data must remain on-premise.

In March 2018, Airbus signed a contract with Google to move its workforce - 130,000 strong at that time - onto the cloud-based Workspace suite. Years later, compatibility issues mean some users still need Excel.

Catherine Jestin, Airbus's executive vice president of digital, told The Register that Google is still working to resolve software interface conflicts between departments and improve features.

Last year, for example, Google tripled the number of cells available in Sheets, which let Airbus "reduce significantly the number of users on Microsoft." This year, Google improved the compatibility of document watermarking. Contracts, for instance, are often exported from Excel to Sheets by one department, edited by another, and sent back.

"Sometimes we had compatibility issues when you were commenting, the comments were lost, or they were not transferable in one way or the other," she said.

"Now they [Google] have made it totally compatible, which means that it makes it much easier for us to decommission people working on contracts… the legal department, the sales department, purchasing department, making it much easier to have that internal collaboration."

Airbus and Google have a roadmap to eliminate Microsoft Office. Jestin expects the migration to finish in "another two to three years."

Not every part of the company will switch. The Defence division, which accounts for the 22 percent (more than 36,000 people), will keep using classic Microsoft Office.

"We will not get 100 percent out of Microsoft because again, for the defense industry people, they cannot [use] Microsoft Office 365 or Google Workspace, they have to stay on-premise, and the only solution that is available on-premise is Microsoft. So Microsoft Office will remain for colleagues who are working in the defense and space programs."

As we pointed out a year ago, kicking the Microsoft habit is no easy feat.  ®

This Rare Stock Market Signal Has Only Happened 5 Other Times Since 1871. Here's What History Says Happens Next.

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The market has seen big pullbacks every time the CAPE ratio has climbed above 30.

By Geoffrey Seiler – Nov 23, 2026 at 4:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

As 2026 winds down, the stock market is set to have another solid year of gains. The S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) is up just a little over 7% on the year, as of the close on Nov. 17. This would be the index's fourth consecutive year of gains if it can finish the year strong.

Now, this year has been a little bit different than past years, with value stocks nicely outperforming growth stocks. This can be seen in the total return performance of the Vanguard Morningstar Growth ETF (VUG -5.32%), which is down about 7.4% for the year, versus the 13.9% gain in the Vanguard Morningstar Value ETF (VTV -1.62%), as of Nov. 22. If this performance rate holds through the end of the year, it would be just the fourth time since 2010 that the Vanguard Value ETF outperformed its growth counterpart.

While the market's leadership has changed this year and there has been some rotation out of artificial intelligence (AI) stocks into other sectors, one thing that hasn't changed is the S&P 500's potentially frothy valuation. One of the best market valuation metrics is the Cyclically Adjusted PE Ratio (CAPE), which was co-developed by Yale economist Robert Shiller. It was designed to help level out periods of unsustainably high and low earnings periods. It does this by measuring the P/E ratio of the S&P 500 based on the index's average inflation-adjusted earnings from the past 10 years.

The CAPE ratio currently sits just below 40 times, one of the highest levels in history. In fact, when the metric is backtested, the CAPE ratio has only been above 30 on five other occasions since 1871, spanning more than 150 years. Meanwhile, history is quite clear about what typically comes next. Let's look at when the CAPE has hit 30 and what the aftermath has been.

History shows that when the CAPE exceeds 30, a big market sell-off tends to follow. However, it sometimes can take years to play out, and in many cases, the market has rebounded pretty quickly.

Importantly, history has also shown that, over the long term, the S&P 500 has consistently risen. As such, even with the CAPE at elevated levels, history shows that the best move is generally to stay the course and be ready to buy stocks on any big pullback.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 719%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 192% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Opinion: If the AI bubble bursts, it could create a serious problem for health care

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

Adler is chief scientific officer at the PATHORA Institute of Pathology & Tissue Medicine.

The Oct. 30 sell-off on Wall Street ignited doubts about whether the huge investment in artificial intelligence will earn the returns investors expect. Those concerns have persisted, with even Anthropic’s initial public offering on Nov. 19 failing to halt the broader tech sell-off.

If the AI bubble bursts (assuming it is a bubble) and takes some medical AI companies down with it, health care will face a serious problem. A health AI company’s market value and the medical value of its technology are two different things. If funding runs out because investors decide to walk away, hospitals could lose the chance to complete evaluations of potentially useful medical tools. Giving up on medical AI would be a financial decision — but it may look like a clinical one. The financial markets will decide which medical AI technologies survive before hospitals have had the opportunity to establish whether these technologies benefit patients.

If investment in medical AI technologies experiences a sharp fall, companies will lose funding, forcing them to reduce their activities or stop supporting their technologies completely. The impact of such an event could be felt across the entire health care system: Companies are developing AI tools ranging from interpreting scans to identifying patients at risk of developing medical complications due to treatments. Clearly, some of these tools will eventually prove useful in clinical settings, while others will not. The point is that neither an impressive AI tool nor a falling stock price tells us which AI tool deserves a place within the health care system.

To illustrate this point, let’s take cancer clinical trials as an example. In one study, researchers from the University of Pennsylvania and Emory University examined electronic medical records using AI systems to identify patients who may meet the eligibility criteria for inclusion in clinical trials. Determining eligibility involves a demanding manual screening process examining things like tumor characteristics, previous treatments, and lab results.

Interestingly, the findings revealed that using the AI system improved accuracy of assessing enrollment eligibility for the cancer clinical trials, but it didn’t reduce screening time significantly. It’s not clear whether such improvements would lead to an increase in patient enrollment. In another study, researchers in Australia from the Victorian Cancer Registry at Cancer Council Victoria and Monash Health utilized an AI system to analyze pathology reports with the goal of identifying patients eligible for enrollment in cancer clinical trials. The AI system was able to successfully identify more eligible patients when compared to standard screening procedures, but ultimately utilizing the AI system didn’t result in increased patient enrollment into the clinical trials. The study didn’t establish why, which brings to focus that screening improvements doesn’t necessarily lead to higher clinical trial participation.

Taken together, these two studies illustrate the potential utility of AI systems in health care. They also make clear that improvements are needed before such AI systems become an integral part of health care system.

Based on my experience in senior leadership in oncology drug development, I think that medical AI systems should be developed and evaluated based on the general framework that we currently apply in the research and development of new innovative cancer drugs. What I mean by that is when it comes to the AI systems promising early results are important, but it is critical not to celebrate a success too early. Ultimately the value of the AI system depends on whether it eventually benefits the patient. We need to determine whether using a particular medical AI leads to better clinical decisions, better patient identification, reduction of unnecessary work, or improved access to treatment.

If the AI bubble bursts, these success metrics could become more difficult to achieve. Companies developing medical AI systems may lose their funding, downsize, or even withdraw support for their products. Worst of all, they could close before hospitals have finished assessing their AI systems.

Clinicians could, of course, return to doing their work as they did before the introduction of the medical AI system. But that would mean the health care system loses the opportunity (and valuable data) to determine whether such medical AI tools could have offered meaningful improvements and patient impact.

The clear answer is not to try and save each struggling AI company, but to make sure that hospitals and health care system are able to complete their assessments of medical AI systems even if a given company reduces its services or even goes out of business.

Before adopting such medical AI systems, hospitals should establish clear success criteria and build systems in place to track, store, and ensure that the research data generated about the medical AI systems are available for independent evaluation even if the AI company supporting such a system no longer exists. Such plans would make sure that any financial difficulties that may affect a relevant AI company would not prevent the hospital or the health care system from determining whether a given medical AI system could benefit patients.

David Adler is chief scientific officer at the PATHORA Institute of Pathology & Tissue Medicine, with more than 15 years of experience in oncology research and drug development.

As diabetes association’s turmoil continues, what does it mean for patients?

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

Liz covers cardiovascular and metabolic disease, asking why we’re not doing better or even losing ground against the world’s leading killer. Send tips on Signal at LizC.22.

Five months after the American Diabetes Association fractured over a political protest and its extended fallout, one question remains unanswered.

What’s the bottom line for people living with diabetes?

Although any impact on patients may be hard to trace now, experts told STAT, they expressed concern that the impasse could stymie advancement in diabetes care. At the heart of the matter is the role the ADA and other professional organizations play in setting guidelines and sharing research, whether in their journals or at wide-ranging meetings that attract thousands to present and to learn.

The ADA dispute began after five members were ousted from its 2026 scientific meeting in New Orleans. The sparring continues, fueled by high-profile resignations and dismissals, a 1,221-member boycott, and the appointment of unidentified editors to manage journals. In October, an internal ADA review acknowledged some missteps but doubled down on its rebukes of members who objected to Trump administration policies decimating scientific research and proposing political reviews of individual projects.

A scheduled appearance by Jay Bhattacharya, director of the National Institutes of Health, inspired the five members to pass out copies of an editorial published in an ADA journal critical of diminished NIH funding. He did not appear, but armed security officers evicted those who shared the printouts outside the hall where he was expected to speak.

Bhattacharya was not the speaker originally suggested for the meeting, Mark Atkinson, who resigned as ADA’s 2026 scientific sessions planning committee chair, told STAT. His choice was to assemble a panel of academic experts to discuss artificial intelligence and its influence on diabetes care, but ADA leadership and its advocacy committee selected Bhattacharya instead.

These and other details regarding the dispute have accumulated, along with high-level resignations. But when asked if the continuing controversy matters to patients, the answer from four prominent voices is yes, but maybe not yet.

To illustrate his concern for the ADA’s future, Atkinson cited examples of how the ADA helped patients in the past.

“The ADA is one of many agencies that have advocated for lower insulin costs,” he told STAT. “They’re one of many agencies that have argued for increased federal funding for research. They’re advocates for standards of care.”

David Nathan, a noted researcher and founder of the Diabetes Center at Massachusetts General Hospital, is far more concerned about the broader impact of federal funding cuts on the scientific enterprise than he is about ADA tumult. Still, he’s also hearing from people who link that ongoing national crisis to what has happened at ADA.

They’re afraid of what’s going to happen to diabetes research, he said, starting with the NIH withdrawing support and then aggravated by the ADA discord.

“It’s really one blow on top of another,” Nathan told STAT. “All of this has coalesced to make everyone anxious, uncertain about the future of diabetes research. And the ADA should at this point be standing up and being something that we can all rally behind. Instead it is being fractured by its own actions.”

In a written response to STAT’s request for comment, the ADA press team said Tuesday, “The ADA has outlined the next steps that we plan to take to learn and improve based on the facts that came to bear from the review. This includes a review of event protocols, procedures, and code of conduct.”

In the near term, the 2027 ADA guidelines to be issued in December were already close to completion, physicians familiar with their production told STAT. The ADA confirmed their publication online in Diabetes Care next month. The agenda for the next annual scientific sessions, slated for June in Washington, D.C., is not yet set.

Meanwhile, the journals have been in limbo.

Since June, the ADA member boycott has erased the editorial masthead for Diabetes Care, its flagship journal. Editor-in-chief Steven Kahn, one of the five escorted out this past June, is part of the boycott, as are deputy editors. Chris Kohler, vice president of publications at ADA, resigned in protest when an interim editorial board was appointed without making their names public.

Kohler’s resignation letter said the appointment of unidentified interim editors violated editorial independence, scientific integrity, transparency, and accountability.

“I cannot, in good conscience, endorse or assume responsibility for an approach that I believe will compound the very problems it is intended to solve,” his Nov. 6 letter said.

Nathan, too, was severe about the unidentified editorial board. “To put it in strike terminology, these are scabs,” Nathan said. “I hate to tarnish them in that way, but they are working against this boycott. And the ADA has not announced the name of a single one of them. That’s weird.”

ADA told STAT the names would be made public.

“While the boycott continues, the ADA will remain relentless in our work to provide the resources that healthcare professionals are counting on. That is why we have established an interim editorial board for Diabetes Care that consists of highly experienced and respected healthcare professionals who have dedicated their careers to caring for people living with diabetes, including endocrinologists, primary care physicians focused on diabetes care, diabetes educators, and more,” the ADA press statement said. “The names of the interim editorial board will be placed on the Diabetes Care website soon.”

Elizabeth Selvin, a deputy editor at Diabetes Care who is also boycotting ADA, expressed concern that a weakened journals might dilute future guidelines.

“I worry that the CEO is treating the journal as a marketing tool for the organization rather than a scientific asset that should be protected and governed by standard publishing ethics,” she wrote to STAT about chief executive Charles Henderson. “A concern is that because typical governance structures to ensure editorial independence are not in place, we need to simply trust that ADA is not making decisions that will benefit industry more than patients.”

In its message to STAT, the ADA said the current editorial team’s term was set to end in December 2027. ADA has opened applications for a new editor-in-chief and said it will work with the selected candidate to confirm a transition plan and the appointment of deputy editors and associate editors.

In addition to the journals, large meetings are another forum for scientific exchange.

John Buse, an endocrinologist at the University of North Carolina and a former president of the ADA, is looking elsewhere.

“I am currently not submitting papers to any ADA journals, but there are many other outstanding journals to pick from,” he wrote to STAT. “It pains me, as of my 600 or so publications, no journal has published more of them than Diabetes Care historically.”

Journals are the way to communicate, Atkinson said.

“Without having medical journals, you don’t have a real way to impact how patients are treated in their care,” he said about the impasse. “This has just got to end soon because the losers in all of this are people with diabetes.”

The same holds true for scientific meetings. Buse plans to attend the Endocrine Society meeting in Toronto, which he has previously gone to only infrequently. He does regularly go to the European Association for the Study of Diabetes, which will meet this year in Budapest, calling it a strong meeting with much to offer. Another option is the American Association of Clinical Endocrinology meeting in San Diego.

“I do hope the issues are resolved soon and that I will attend the ADA, but the plan today is not to attend the scientific sessions in D.C. in June,” he wrote. “If the negotiations with ADA go nowhere and the boycott persists, there is great risk that the ADA loses its standing as the authoritative voice in diabetes. I am sure that the Endocrine Society, AACE, EASD, and others could carry the mantle in the future if the diaspora of thought leaders persists.”

Nathan said he and his colleagues are also thinking about where to present the results of their research, predicting scientists will find other venues. And so will participants and sponsors.

“Industry is interested in having a large audience at these annual meetings. If they start leaving, or if their support starts weakening, the ADA will suffer from it,” Nathan said. “As will people with diabetes. As will the scientific enterprise. I mean, this is just a colossal blunder on the part of the ADA.”

The ADA said there are plans to meet with some of the people leading the boycott to work towards a resolution.”

“They seem to be procrastinating, frankly, with us,” he said. “We have tried to get a meeting with them for easily more than four months. I think they’re hoping that this will just go away, and it’s not going away.”

STAT’s coverage of chronic health issues is supported by a grant from Bloomberg Philanthropies. Our financial supporters are not involved in any decisions about our journalism.
