Activision patents AI voice system that learns from in-game chat and creates audio for NPCs who can even speak in place of you

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Call of Duty publisher Activision has filed for a new patent allowing it to train NPCs on real-world player voice chat. The patent describes an AI-driven system that listens in on player voice chat following key gameplay moments and later recreates it using in-game characters.

The patent, spotted by Patentlyze, was filed in July and published in November. According to the filing and its diagrams, the system is a self-learning neural network that recognises major gameplay moments - such as kills, longshots, deaths and so on - and records the player's reaction and voice chat from the moment just before and then after they happen. These can be as simple as brief celebratory shouts, groans or yells, or as complex as trash talk.

The system compares those captured player reactions and then correlates them with coinciding gameplay events in order to understand their significance, essentially learning as it goes what triggers certain reactions. The AI then teaches itself to quickly identify those when they occur in-game and replicate them.

Although the replicated voice lines don't directly repeat players' own chat, the system does keep a profile of each player it analyses so it can deliver a response tailored to them, including sticking to the same spoken language, which it uses large language models (LLMs) to help identify. The patent describes a filter of sorts to keep NPCs from learning offensive language such as slurs.

According to the patent, the system's reach extends beyond in-game voice chat to also potentially access voice comms from major apps like Discord.

Activision's goal is to create NPCs that can speak naturally, rather than re-use the same pre-recorded lines. The application seems to be limited to multiplayer games, including those with co-op. Think of the generic voice lines spoken by AI-teammates like "cover me" and so on in a PvE game, or the many other similar ones in battle royale and PvP matches that contain dozens or hundreds of players.

Incidentally, the patent talks of replacing those generic messages with learned ones for players who join a match without a microphone, but players can also seemingly allow their in-game avatars to always use generated voices.

As the game industry continues to grapple with the use of generative-AI in game development, Activision's patent seems to be going directly to source, recreating how those LLMs and AI systems got their knowledge to begin with: from humans.

Mass General Brigham spinout Aevius raises $22m to make medicine computable

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Aevius’s four co-founders all helped build Modella AI, which AstraZeneca bought in January. Eric Topol, the cardiologist and digital medicine researcher, chairs its scientific advisory board.

Aevius co-founders, from left: Long Phi Le, Rowland Pettit, Ming Yang (Max) Lu and Faisal Mahmood

Aevius, a Boston clinical AI startup spun out of Mass General Brigham, has raised $22m led by Insight Partners. The company announced the round on 17 November in a press release. Chief executive Rowland Pettit told Axios Pro it is a seed round.

The money will go on model and product development, hiring and commercial deployment, the company said. It plans to add engineering, clinical and commercial staff. It did not give a valuation or name any customers.

“Our mission is to make medicine computable,” Pettit, a physician-scientist and co-founder, said in the release.

Pettit founded Aevius with chief technology officer Ming Yang (Max) Lu, Faisal Mahmood and Long Phi Le. All four were part of the founding team behind Modella AI, a cancer-focused medical AI startup. AstraZeneca bought Modella in January, as Brian Gormley reported for The Wall Street Journal. The drugmaker already uses AI to find trial patients.

Eric Topol, the cardiologist and digital medicine researcher, chairs Aevius’s scientific advisory board.

“The value of medical AI will be measured by how well it promotes accuracy,” Topol said in the release.

Aevius builds AI models that learn from structured, unstructured and specialty medical data. From a patient’s records, they build a refreshable multimodal patient representation, in the company’s words. It is a computable picture of the patient’s condition and trajectory that updates as new information arrives.

Organisations can build several applications on that one representation, the company said. They can forecast outcomes, find clinically similar patients, sort patients into groups and track change over time. Aevius is selling to health systems, payers and life sciences companies.

“Most AI agents today piece together their picture of a patient from text, one question at a time,” Lu said in the release.

He said Aevius instead builds a native representation of each patient’s whole history. Researchers, care teams and AI agents can then take on new questions without starting over.

Mass General Brigham said Aevius brings together research developed at the hospital system with a team that has taken medical AI to market. Dylan Morris, a managing director at Insight Partners, said clinical data is among the hardest kinds of data to put to use. Insight has also backed AI recruiting startup Metaview and sales tax startup Numeral.

Need to solve a problem? Neuroscience says try the Jack Reacher Method

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There’s a point in Lee Child’s book Make Me (the 20th in his Jack Reacher series, for those keeping score) where Reacher is trying to solve a problem and decides to back-burner it: Instead of actively working the problem, he sets it aside, trusting his subconscious to eventually make the right connections.

Which it does.

Sound kinda hokey?

Actually, no. Neuroscience shows you tend to be at your most creative when you’re not trying to be creative. Actually, you’re at your most creative when you’re not trying to do anything at all.

Researchers at Washington University in St. Louis used PET scans to determine which parts of the brain lit up during different types of activity. To establish baselines, some participants were put in the scanner and told to do nothing, what the researchers called “resting state.” Surprisingly, resting state resulted in mental energy spikes across various brain regions.

In fact, mental energy turned out to be higher in resting state than active state, where participants were given mental tasks.

A few years later, neuroscientist Nancy Andreasen made resting-state activity (she called it “free floating periods of thought”) her focus. No tasks. No inputs. No intentional creativity. No nothing; just vegging. 

As Andreasen writes in The Creating Brain: The Neuroscience of Genius:

Most investigators made the convenient assumption that the brain would be blank or neutral during “rest.” From introspection I knew that my own brain is often at its most active when I stretch out on a bed or sofa and close my eyes.

We found activations in multiple regions of the association cortex. We were not [seeing] a passive silent brain during the resting state, but rather a brain that was actively connecting thoughts and experiences.

Andreasen termed that activity REST: random episodic silent thinking. During REST, Andreasen says, your brain “uses its most human and complex parts, areas known to gather information and link it all together, in potentially novel ways.”

That’s why you sometimes get ideas while taking a walk. Or taking a shower. Or doing dishes. Or driving. When you let your mind wander, when you don’t actively focus on anything specific, your brain makes all sorts of seemingly unconnected associations.

You think about the future. You think about the past. You speculate. You synthesize. You test and discard. 

As Andreasen writes, “When the brain/mind thinks in a free and unencumbered fashion, it uses its most human and complex parts.”

In simple terms, if you want to be creative, stop trying to be creative. Stop actively trying to work the problem. Do something relatively mindless, and let your mind do some work in the background.

As Steven Johnson wrote in The New York Times:

The seemingly trivial activity of mind-wandering is now believed to play a central role in the brain’s “deep learning,” the mind’s sifting through past experiences, imagining future prospects, and assessing them with emotional judgments: that flash of shame or pride or anxiety that each scenario elicits.

Granted, REST, or mind-wandering, or whatever you want to call it, is easier said than done.

For one thing, stimulation and “focus” are always within easy reach. Plus, it can feel unproductive, and even oddly uncomfortable, not to fill time with some sort of activity.

How many people sit in a waiting room and just wait? How many people sit on a plane and just stare at the seatback in front of them? How many people take a walk without listening to something? How many people don’t listen to something while they’re driving? How many people work out without earbuds? (While it’s just one data point, the other day I went to the gym and counted 18 of 20 people wearing headphones. One of the two not wearing headphones was me.)

Sitting and thinking in a “free and unencumbered fashion”? Sounds boring.

And it is boring, at least at first. It’s also tough to stick with, especially if you decide to sit in a chair and just stare out the window.

So try this instead. Take a walk. (Then you’ll be double-dipping: A study published in the Journal of Experimental Psychology found that your creative output increases by as much as 60% when you’re walking.)

Just walk. Don’t think about thinking. Don’t think about what you should be doing, should be listening to, should be focused on. Let your mind wander. 

In all likelihood, your thoughts will soon drift to a problem you have. Or a challenge you’re working through. Your thoughts will shift forward and backward in time. You’ll sift through hypothetical possibilities and outcomes.

You’ll actively, without trying, connect ideas, thoughts, and experiences. 

When you’re REST-ing, you won’t have to come up with ideas. Ideas will come to you, because that’s how your brain is wired.

—By Jeff Haden

The stock market faces a worrying scenario: a sell-off with no flight to safety

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Good morning. U.S. markets are in correction territory, with the S&P 500 down more than 11% since its October highs, and the Nasdaq Composite down about 18%. Take your pick of culprits: AI spending, the Nvidia turmoil, trade tensions, the midterm fallout, sticky inflation.

While S&P 500 futures suggest there could be a bounce today, stocks could be volatile for some time. Here’s what to watch:

The AI ecosystem. Nvidia shares kept sliding, but the pain also spread to stocks like Caterpillar, which now trades like an AI infrastructure stock because so much of its growth comes from data centers. The company’s stock fell 5% Friday and is down about 28% over the past month. Data center opposition was a winning issue in several midterm races, meaning future deals could face more friction. For those who supply cooling equipment, power generation and other data center infrastructure, the question is which companies already have financing and approval. Nvidia’s deals with specialized AI cloud providers, or neoclouds, are contingent on those customers securing financing. And Nvidia itself is backing deals, though the details are often fuzzy, making it hard to predict how Nvidia’s lower valuation ripples through customers whose deals depend on its backing.

The bond market. When investors flee stocks, they often shift more money into the “safe haven” of bonds, which pushes up Treasury prices and pushes down yields, the interest rates on those bonds. The 10-year Treasury yield has eased, but it’s still hovering near 5% (with mortgage rates still above 7.3%), which concerns investors like Bruce K. Lee, the founder and CEO of Keebeck Wealth Management. “There should be a flight to safety and I don’t see it,” he told me over the weekend. “If bond yields continue to stay high and equity prices fall, that creates a problem.” In other words, when investors don’t treat Treasuries as a safe haven, borrowing costs stay high for companies and homebuyers even as stock portfolios shrink.

Lee also wonders whether the Fed has lost some of its tools. It’s battling to get inflation down amid concern about the record $40 trillion national debt and the diminished appetite of others to hold it. Purchases of Treasuries by America’s trading partners are down 60% in the first eight months of 2026, compared to the same period a year earlier. Japan, now the biggest foreign holder of Treasuries, cut its buying by 99% while South Korea and Taiwan were both net sellers. With foreign demand for Treasuries drying up, the Fed has less room to maneuver. Washington’s trade war could be one factor. “The people that you’re claiming war against are also our customers who buy our Treasuries,” said Lee. “I always say sell, don’t yell.”

The economic outlook. For months, a buoyant stock market and solid GDP growth have curbed fears of 1970s-style stagflation, in which stagnant economic growth destroys jobs while high inflation erodes purchasing power. The stock market is just one indicator for the U.S. economy, but it’s an important barometer of faith in good times and solid profits to come. Was Friday the start of a bear market, a correction within a bull market, or a brief pause before another stock market boom?  That’s for investors to decide. While consumer sentiment rose after the midterms, it remains low as many struggle to buy basic necessities and housing remains out of reach. The AI revolution is real, but its gains are choppy and could be distributed in ways that are hard to predict.

Contact CEO Daily via Diane Brady at diane.brady@fortune.com

In August, Patrick and John Collison, Stripe’s co-founders, announced that “the world had entered the singularity” as of Jan. 1. The Stripe cofounders aren’t the only tech executives talking about the singularity–a hypothesized turning point when AI drives technological change faster than humans can keep up. Yet companies are increasingly relying on AI to oversee these changes, potentially making human oversight even harder.

Whoever succeeds Jensen Huang at Nvidia will face one of business’s most notoriously difficult jobs: Following a founder. Huang built the chip giant from the ground up, earned fierce loyalty from employees, and came to personify the company itself. Even after they leave the company, founders tend to linger in one capacity or another, making it hard for a replacement to chart their own path.

Inside the race to put computers in human brains

Neuralink’s brain implant is making science fiction a reality for the approximately 26 people testing the device today. The first such implant to become commercially available in the U.S. will be a watershed moment for the industry, setting off what some envision to be a decades-long process to merge our minds with machines. But the technology also raises a fundamental question about data privacy: Do we want to give employers or governments access to what’s happening inside our heads?

S&P 500 futures are up 2.5% this morning. The last session closed down 3.7%. South Korea’s KOSPI fell by 3.3%, while Hong Kong’s Hang Seng was down by 0.1%. Taiwan’s TAIEX rose by 2.1% after regulators introduced emergency measures to stabilize the market. Japan’s markets are closed today. India’s NIFTY 50 is currently down 1.4%, while the STOXX Europe 600 is up 0.6% in early trading. Bitcoin is back above $61,000, reversing a weekend plunge.

The BMW iX5 Was Driven Until It Died. It Had A Surprising Amount Of Hidden Range Left

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

The BMW iX5 is a big deal for the German automaker. It’s the company’s first electric X5, a model that has traditionally been one of its best-sellers, and it’s also the first electric BMW assembled in the United States.

In other words, the iX5 has a lot on its shoulders, so it has to deliver. Thankfully, the automaker crammed a massive, 144-kilowatt-hour battery under the SUV’s body, and now a new test from Out of Spec Testing reveals just how much range is hidden in the EV’s pack, even after the instrument cluster shows there’s nothing more to give.

The test, which was performed on a closed course, started when the battery still had 4% energy left. The car’s displays said that the driver should recharge at the next opportunity, which makes a lot of sense in the real world, but the SUV still drove normally. At 2% state of charge, the car’s brain limited the power output to roughly 350 horsepower, down from approximately 450 hp, but that’s still plenty.

At 1%, the iX5 still delivered plenty of performance, and the driver still managed to maintain speeds between 35 and 55 miles per hour. The same speeds were still possible when the battery level reached zero, but a turtle icon appeared on the dash, accompanied by a yellow power meter and a warning message telling the driver to avoid heavy acceleration and overtaking.

All this being said, the iX5 did not slow down. It soldiered on, covering 8.4 miles after the battery level had reached 0% and consuming almost 3 kWh from the battery that was supposedly empty. The pedal started to feel a little softer at the 10-mile mark, and the first big power cut came at 10.7 miles, when the car stopped taking accelerator inputs and started slowing down.

But that wasn’t the end of the test. The iX5 was restarted and, surprisingly, continued driving at speeds of up to 55 mph. After consuming 7.4 kWh from the reserve, the iX5 eventually shut down, slowing down to a standstill and automatically engaging Park. It drove a total of 21.8 miles after the instrument cluster first showed that the battery had 0% energy left.

A second restart attempt was unsuccessful, but it goes to show that BMW put quite a big buffer on the iX5’s battery. It also means that the SUV’s total battery capacity is somewhere around 151 kWh.

Range-wise, the 2027 BMW iX5 60 xDrive, the only version currently available, can drive up to 456 miles on a full battery, according to the EPA. In a recent comparison with the Lucid Gravity, Porsche Cayenne, and Rivian R1S, the iX5 covered 411 miles at 70 mph, beating all of its competitors by at least 43 miles.

Zelda Switch 2 restock: Live restock updates at Walmart, Target, and Best Buy (Nov. 23)

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If your current morning ritual involves violently hitting refresh on a dozen retail tabs, you already know the vibe: finding the Nintendo Switch 2: The Legend of Zelda 40th Anniversary Edition is turning into an Olympic sport.

We're closely monitoring inventory at all the major retailers and are cautiously optimistic it will be restocked for the holiday shopping season. On the question of if/when the limited-edition Zelda Switch 2 will be restocked, a Nintendo spokesperson provided this not-entirely-reassuring statement to Mashable: "Although we cannot provide detailed information, we are striving to ensure that we can supply our products to those who want them."

Between automated bot armies scooping digital drops, surprise order cancellations at Walmart, and inventory vanishing in seconds on Amazon, this custom black-and-gold console feels like a myth. But while digital storefronts look completely barren, stock is actively circulating behind the scenes.

Before getting into the restock details, bookmark these retailer pages to check for surprise drops:

Nintendo Store — Out of stock right now, but you can add it to your wishlist to get pinged the second a restock hits.

Amazon — Dropping tiny batches of consoles randomly throughout the day.

Walmart — Stock exists but only for third-party listings selling at way over market price, so make sure your in-stock email alerts are turned on.

Target — Sold out online, but periodic drops have taken place in-store.

Best Buy — Sold out online, but select stores ran midnight openings on Nov. 5 with walk-in stock.

GameStop — Online orders are off; you will have to walk into a store to find one.

The feeding frenzy is being supercharged by Nintendo’s staggered release calendar. While the custom Zelda 40th Anniversary hardware dropped first on Oct. 29, the flagship game driving demand — The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time for Nintendo Switch 2 — arrived on Thursday, Nov. 5.

The remake rebuilds the 1998 classic from the ground up with modernized controls, a fully orchestrated soundtrack, reworked dungeon puzzles, and expanded Hyrule environments. Since the $520 console doesn’t include the game, everyone is rushing to lock down a system before launch day.

The package includes the special-edition Switch 2 system sporting a custom black-and-gold finish, themed Joy-Con 2 controllers, and a matching dock decorated with the gold Triforce emblem. It retains the platform's signature flexibility, letting you switch between TV, Tabletop, and Handheld modes whenever you want. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Inside the themed chassis, you get 256GB of built-in storage (expandable with microSD Express cards), a vivid display in handheld mode, faster load times, and support for up to 4K resolution on compatible TVs when the console sits in the dock.

The magnetic Joy-Con 2 controllers add mouse-style tracking in supported games, while the new GameChat hub lets you voice chat and share your screen directly as you play. It also plays your older Switch games and supports standard multiplayer options.

Landing limited-edition Nintendo hardware takes a little prep work, but these battle-tested habits will give you a real edge over the bots:

Save your checkout details everywhere — Log in to Target, Best Buy, Walmart, and the Nintendo Store to save your shipping address and default card. Stopping to type a card number or billing zip code during a live drop is how you lose an order.

Hands off the refresh button in Best Buy's queue — When an online drop goes live, Best Buy puts shoppers into a randomized waiting line to filter out scrapers. Touching the reload button resets your spot and boots you straight to the back.

Watch for Walmart+ early-access paywalls — Walmart frequently locks headline console drops behind paid Walmart+ subscriptions for the first 30 to 60 minutes. If you have a membership, keep that tab signed in; if not, wait for the general public window to unlock.

Dodge third-party sellers — Double-check the seller before buying. Marketplace scalpers frequently list units for jacked-up prices; make sure it says "Sold and shipped by [Retailer]" at the standard price.

Skip the web wars and head to physical stores — Online checkout bots grab website stock in under 15 seconds. Showing up in person is easily your most reliable path to securing one without paying scalper markups.

Stores like Walmart, Amazon, and Best Buy often hold onto popular gaming hardware to use as headline deals during Black Friday and Cyber Monday week.

With that said, Black Friday restocks are usually the hardest ones to catch. You will be fighting website crashes, gift shoppers, and automated bots all at once. If you can grab a console whenever stock appears, take it. Think of Black Friday as a backup plan, not your main bet.

Console hunting sucks right now, period. But between the surprise micro-drops and physical store restocks, you still have real shots at retail price — just be ready to move the second that stock goes live.

Elon Musk Backs Binance Founder's Use of Grok AI Assistant

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Elon Musk has reacted to a post by Binance co-founder Changpeng Zhao (CZ), in which the latter shared that he used Grok Bot to tidy up his X following list.

According to CZ, the bot was tasked with removing inactive and abusive accounts from the list of people he follows, though accounts with constructive criticism were supposed to stay. CZ was not fully sure about the results, adding "AI did it" with a shrug emoji.

Musk replied with "Cool." Later, CZ added in the same thread that it "worked well so far."

Back in September, CZ had to address rumors about his account being hacked after users noticed that he had unfollowed a number of profiles. Per CZ, at that time he removed accounts that were inactive for more than 30 days.

Grok Bot, the agent product by Musk's xAI, was launched on Aug. 11, with X integration added at the end of that month. The bots run on separate cloud computers and can log in to a user's apps and accounts.

Since then, the product has received praise from Nvidia head Jensen Huang, who said in October that Grok Bot usage was "really skyrocketing." Musk himself claimed on SpaceX's Q3 earnings call that he had never seen a product grow this fast.

On Coinbase, Grok was responsible for 59.54% of AI-agent trading volume from Sept. 14 to Sept. 21. For comparison, Claude had 13.37% and ChatGPT only 1.54%. Coinbase CEO Brian Armstrong then named Grok the leading client for agentic traders on the platform, to which Musk also responded with "Cool."

At the same time, there were complaints. NewCore researchers reported last week that Grok Bot made 31 unrequested access attempts to email, calendar, Slack and GitHub accounts on two employee laptops within five days, ignoring the command to stop.

It was only last month that Musk was trolling Binance after the exchange lost about $6.3 billion in a hack on Oct. 29, the largest one in crypto history so far. Among other things, Musk posted "Everyday is Zeroday" and replied with fire and laughing emojis to CZ's earlier warning about phishing links.

CZ then asked Musk to stop, calling it a "bad time to troll," and reminded him that Binance's $500 million investment in Twitter in 2022 has become a stake in SpaceX worth more than $1 billion.

Now, the two seem to be on good terms again.

Topics Amazon Apple Black Friday Gadgets Kitchen

This year, Black Friday falls on Nov. 27. While retailers often start featuring their best markdowns on Black Friday itself, there are plenty of early deals worth shopping in the lead-up. Most retailers' official sales do start well before the big day, so shopping strategically ahead of time can save you from missing out on a deal entirely (or dealing with the chaos the day after Thanksgiving).

Cyber Monday takes place on the Monday following Thanksgiving. This year, the shopping event takes place on Monday, Nov. 30. While Cyber Monday originally began as an online counterpart to in-person shopping on Black Friday, the two events have merged into an extended online shopping bonanza.

The mega-retailer just wrapped up its October Prime Day sale, and naturally, it's also getting in on the Black Friday action. For 2026, Amazon's official Black Friday sales kicked off on Nov. 19, which means deals are already live. Amazon has an incentive to spread out the shopping (to give its delivery drivers a break), so you no longer have to wait until Thanksgiving to save. Deals will continue to go live through Nov. 30

Amazon: Amazon recently announced that its Black Friday sale will begin on Nov. 19, with new deals dropping through Cyber Monday, on Nov. 30.

Best Buy: The big box store is hosting its Early Black Friday Deals + Doorbusters sale from Nov. 6 through Nov. 18, and the official Best Buy Black Friday sale kicks off on Nov. 19 and runs through Nov. 28. The Best Buy Cyber Monday Sale Event runs for 48 hours on Nov. 29 and Nov. 30.

Walmart: Walmart has three major sale events throughout November: the early Black Friday sale from Nov. 12 to Nov. 15, the Black Friday sale from Nov. 23 to Nov. 29 (with deals online only from Nov. 23 to Nov. 26), and the online-only Cyber Monday sale from Nov. 30 to Dec. 2.

Target: Target hasn't released its full Black Friday lineup yet, but we do know its first Early Black Friday sale kicks off Nov. 5 to Nov. 7. From Nov. 1 to Dec. 24, Target's Deal of the Day offer kicks off, featuring new deals every day. Every weekend after Nov. 7, the retailer will also drop new limited-time holiday offers.

10 podcast episodes that will make you think differently about work and life

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This article is republished with permission from Wonder Tools, a newsletter that helps you discover the most useful sites and apps.

Today I’m sharing nine of my favorite recent episodes.

First, my podcast tool kit

I listen with the Snipd app, which lets me mark favorite moments. I triple-tap my AirPods or Sony XM6 headphones to highlight an excerpt. (Why I use Snipd.)

Snipd then emails me a summary of my highlights. Readwise hosts my collected podcast notes along with everything else I’ve highlighted while reading online or on my Kindle.

Later, I query my clips with Claude or ChatGPT, using the Snipd and Readwise connectors, known as MCPs. (Here’s how that works.) This approach helps me find passages I liked and pull quotes like the ones below.

1. Time-Sensitive

Host: Spencer Bailey
Standout episode: Oliver Burkeman on the Power of Embracing Imperfectionism

I love this podcast’s focus on time. Bailey picks thoughtful guests and asks probing, surprising questions. His thorough research leads to questions other interviewers wouldn’t think to pose.

Oliver Burkeman is one of my favorite contemporary authors. I’ve adopted ideas from each of his books, and I make a point of listening to his podcast interviews. So I appreciated that this conversation with Bailey felt different, like the two were venturing into new territory.

In addition to covering Burkeman’s early career writing for The Guardian, they discussed why we shouldn’t obsess over others’ requests for our time, and how to make peace with imperfection.

Bailey is founder of the Slowdown, a collective of writers, editors, photographers, and others focused on quality over quantity.

Another great episode: Maria Popova on the role of chance in shaping our lives. The intro to the podcast sums up why Popova’s such a good guest: “The Bulgarian-born, Brooklyn-based . . . founder of the ‘free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human’ website and newsletter The Marginalian braids together literature, science, philosophy, poetry, and art.”

2. How I Work

Host: Dr. Amantha Imber
Standout episode: Feel Stuck in Small Talk? Daniel Coyle Shares the Questions That Create Real Connection

I discovered Daniel Coyle through this episode of Dr. Amantha Imber’s great podcast. He overflows with useful ideas. One of his books, The Talent Code, explores how top performers excel. In a video I like, he summarizes some of its most interesting takeaways.

In his conversation with Imber, Coyle offers practical ways to move beyond small talk: Ask better questions, try small community rituals, and notice what gives people energy.

3. Curious Minds at Work

Host: Gayle Allen
Standout episode: David Epstein on the Power of Limits

David Epstein references Dr. Seuss and Apple legend Tony Fadell. His anecdotes spotlight historical figures and contemporary ones. In this interview he draws on themes from his latest book, Inside the Box: How Constraints Make Us Better, to explain how limits help with creativity.

Allen is a superb interviewer who asks concise questions and avoids putting herself at the center of the conversation. Subscribe to her podcast for interviews with top contemporary nonfiction writers.

Notable quote: “Useful constraints . . . force you to clarify priorities.” —David Epstein

4. The Happiness Lab

Host: Dr. Laurie Santos
Standout episode: How to Stop Work From Taking Over Your Life

I struggle to stop working sometimes, so this episode gave me a lot to ponder. I liked psychologist Guy Winch’s suggestion to treat dreaded tasks like pebbles in our shoes and remove them right away. Thinking of them as nuisances may be helpful in combating procrastination. The episode also suggests planning restorative micro-breaks and building an end-of-work ritual.

Notable quote: “We’re taking the 15-minute task, say, 15 minutes of unpleasantness, and we’re smearing it over an entire week.” —Guy Winch

5. The Knowledge Project

Host: Shane Parrish
Standout episode: Morgan Housel: Wealth Is What You Have Minus What You Want

Like Ezra Klein’s, Parrish’s podcast is excellent partly because of the host’s expertise and partly because he picks great guests. Morgan Housel, the interviewee on this episode, is an entertaining storyteller whose first and third books are about the psychology of money. I liked his second most: Same as Ever: A Guide to What Never Changes.

Read four of the key points from this episode, or see the full show.

6. Acquired

Hosts: Ben Gilbert and David Rosenthal
Standout episode: Trader Joe’s

Each episode of Acquired explores a single company. Episodes are three to four hours long. The hosts say they spend 100-plus hours doing deep research on each of the eight episodes they publish per year.

The depth and quality of their research, reporting, and storytelling have drawn in more than a million listeners. Sponsors pay $500,000 and up, according to a profile of Acquired in The New York Times. There’s even a Harvard Business School case study about their rise.

Their marathon episodes challenge the idea that consumers want only short media. This Trader Joe’s episode lasts three hours and 26 minutes. That’s longer than many shoppers spend in the store all year.

Listening to Acquired feels like reading an interesting book. The hosts weave lively details about each company they cover into a conversational narrative. Their sharp analysis helps explain how successful ventures stand out.

They detail why Trader Joe’s avoids coupons, ads, and sales; how its founder scouted locations for his first stores and overcame early cash-flow problems; and how the company avoided risky expansions overseas and online.

UK defence startup Agon raises $70M to simulate the battlefield

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The London company, now valued at $450M, builds virtual testing grounds for drones and other military robots.

British defence startup Agon has raised $70M to expand its software for testing military robots, valuing it at $450M. The pace is striking, as the company was founded in January and it came out of stealth earlier this year with $30M in funding.

Bloomberg first reported the round, which Northzone led with Lakestar, Lux Capital, and XYZ alongside. The UK’s Sovereign AI Fund and its National Security Strategic Investment Fund joined too.

So did two big-name angels: Roelof Botha, the former head of Sequoia Capital, and Unity co-founder David Helgason, who chairs Agon’s board.

Agon builds what it calls a synthetic battlefield. The software lets weapon makers test how their drones, sea vessels, and other robots behave in a given setting.

The idea, much like a simulator for self-driving cars, is to give robots a safe place to make their mistakes.

The new capital will pay for AI models that can run those tests in many more settings. And Agon’s plans don’t stop at its own product.

“We’re going to expand to effectively build out Europe’s defence AI lab,” co-founder and CEO Tristam Constant told Bloomberg, adding that this will be an expensive undertaking.

Constant previously worked at Applied Intuition, one of the simulation companies Agon now competes with. His co-founder Junaid Hussain helped set up Cambridge Aerospace, the British anti-missile startup valued at $3.4bn in August.

Investors have rushed into European defence tech since Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine set off a spending surge.

This year they have put a record $7.4bn into the sector’s startups, according to October data from Dealroom and Resilience Media, and Stark Defence‘s €500M round was among the biggest.

In that crowd, Agon pitches itself as the neutral one. It arms nobody, but gives every weapons maker a place to put its machines through their paces.

‘MoldyRoost’ ads can steal crypto wallet recovery phrases from iPhones

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The campaign used at least 480 ad domains to target users in more than 47 countries and could infect a vulnerable iPhone with a single tap.

Hackers are using fake ads to spread malware that can steal crypto wallet recovery phrases from vulnerable iPhones, cybersecurity company Confiant said Friday.

Confiant, which calls the campaign MoldyRoost, linked at least 480 ad domains to it between May 12 and Nov. 17. The ads targeted users in more than 47 countries.

“Just loading the landing page of a MoldyRoost ad on a vulnerable iPhone is enough to get infected,” Confiant said in its report.

Google Threat Intelligence reported in March that hackers were using a tool called Coruna to target visitors to fake crypto sites. MoldyRoost bought ads to spread the same tool on sites unrelated to crypto, where tapping an ad could expose a recovery phrase saved on a vulnerable iPhone.

Coruna can search Apple Notes for wallet seed phrases and read QR codes in saved images. A thief who gets a seed phrase can use those words to open the wallet on another device and move the funds.

The campaign also used DarkSword, another tool that can steal wallet data, which Google researchers also warned of in March.

In a Nov. 6 warning about DarkSword, blockchain security firm CertiK urged iPhone users to update their software and keep seed phrases out of Notes, photos and screenshots. It also advised avoiding unfamiliar links and using a hardware wallet for larger holdings.

Elegoo's Centauri 2 Combo delivers multi-color 3D printing on a budget — but you might miss the enclosure

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elegoo's Centauri 2 Combo delivers very solid print quality and four-color printing for under $380. Inevitably given that low price, certain corners are cut. None of these are deal-breakers, but there are better printers on the market — including quite a few from Elegoo — for only a little bit more cash. Still, if you're on a tight budget or are a beginner who is interested in multi-color printing, this is a decent choice.

Multi-color printing generates a lot of waste

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

3D printing has been edging closer and closer to the mainstream for years now. It's cheaper than ever to buy a really good, feature-packed machine, while the various manufacturers are constantly working to try and make their products easier to use and more accessible.

Despite that, it can still feel like a comparative niche to newcomers, and getting to grips with your first 3D printer can be... intimidating. Enter Elegoo's Centauri line, which has been around since 2024. These are designed to bring 3D printing to the masses, with easy to use machines at affordable prices.

The Centauri Carbon 2 Combo launched at the start of the year and was a very impressive model for its $449 RRP, but the company has recently upped the ante with an even-more-budget offering that does most of what its predecessor can do for under $380 — and you can already find it cheaper than that in some of the sales. But does that low price point come with any downsides?

Find out in my full Elegoo Centauri 2 Combo review.

It really is quite impressive what this printer can do. It delivers easy setup, good build quality, and multi-color 3D printing at such a low price point.

The most obvious selling point of the Centauri 2 Combo is its four-color printing. You simply attach the spool holders to the top of the machine, pop on your filament and then feed them into each extruder.

If you're using official Elegoo spools with RFID tags, then the CANVAS unit (Elegoo’s filament switching system) on the top of the machine will automatically identify which color is which. You then simply feed the filament into the numbered inlets on the machine. The results are good, with well-defined color changes. My first print was a classic Benchy, which printed quickly and cleanly in 45 minutes.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Next up, I printed two Daleks from Doctor Who, based on a nice fan-made model that I found on Cults3D by user wayne_peters.

The red one was printed using the factory standard printer settings. For the yellow I tweaked several settings for surface ironing and adaptive layers in the hope of getting a smoother print. That significantly upped the print time, but didn't deliver massively different results. If you’re noticing some rough edges, those were me being a bit slapdash post print, not an issue with the printer.

One thing to note is that printing models with multiple colors will significantly increase the amount of waste filament produced, because of the way the printer has to switch between colors. That's not a criticism of the Centauri 2 Combo, it's just the nature of the technology. If you are concerned about filament wastage then printing models with multi-colored parts may not be for you.

The Centauri 2 Combo arrived securely packaged and was very easy to assemble. I'd say it was a seamless experience, except for one small but really quite frustrating aspect that added a good 15 minutes of swearing onto the build.

That was attaching the filament hub. You have to fix it into place with two tiny screws, one of which feels almost impossible to get into position because of the way the cable drag chain is positioned. I really struggled with this, almost losing the screw several times, before finally somehow managing to get it into place.

Thankfully after that, everything ran perfectly smoothly. When you first switch the machine on it runs through a self-check and calibration, as well as levelling the bed automatically. That really takes the stress out of the setup and is another reason why this is a good machine for beginners.

After setup, you need to install two pieces of software: ElegooSlicer on your computer and Elegoo Matrix on a smartphone.

ElegooSlicer is the software used to work on your 3D models (which can be downloaded from sites like Printables, Makerworld, and Elegoo's own Nexprint) before exporting them ready to be printed. It's based on the open-source OrcaSlicer and is intuitive enough for newcomers to get to grips with quickly.

Elegoo Matrix enables you to monitor and control your printer remotely and is pretty fool proof. Again, you get the sense of a system that has been designed with novices in mind.

I can't stress how straightforward this printer is, as it does most of the hard work for you automatically. If this is your first 3D printer then it won't take you very long to get used to it, and if you've used one of Elegoo's other printers before, particularly any of the Centauri models, then you'll have no worries

I printed the Halloween skeleton candlestick below, found on Nexprint, in a little over seven hours — pretty good for such an intricate design!

A curvy vase, also from Nexprint, was equally straightforward and printed in just over three hours, with no need for the supports that you would typically need to include with complex prints. I've since used the machine to print out some gaming terrain as well as for practical applications like paint storage for my Warhammer paints and storage boxes.

Over several weeks of use and around 30 prints I had only one fail. I cancelled the print, tried again and the second time it worked with no problems.

Despite the low price point, the Centauri 2 Combo doesn't feel cheap. There are some cut corners — we'll come to those in a moment — but it's a reassuringly solid piece of kit, robustly built from a die-cast aluminum frame and steel body. Once again, it recalls Elegoo's bestselling Centauri 2 Combo Carbon in its design, which feels similarly solid.

The build-plate is double-sided (textured on side A and smooth on side B) and made from magnetic spring steel that's easy to keep clean. The toolhead comes with a standard 0.4mm hardened steel nozzle, which can be swapped out for different sizes, which are sold separately. You will also need to buy a nozzle wrench to do that.

It feels almost churlish to complain about the Centauri 2 Combo as it's really very good at what it sets out to do — and there's no arguing with that aggressive price point. Even so, there are a few reasons why you might want to consider shelling out for a slightly more expensive machine.

Probably the biggest talking point with the Centauri 2 Combo is the price — but it comes at the cost of several features that you'd usually find on a 3D printer.

The most obvious of these omissions is the lack of an enclosure. The panels, glass door and hood are all absent, meaning that the print bed is exposed for all to see. In this way it echoes the design of several competitors, including Anycubic's Kobra X and Creality's 3D SparkX i7 Combo. That said, I much prefer the neat and tidy design of the Centauri 2 Combo.

There's something quite cool about being able to watch your latest print materialise right there in front of you without it being hidden away behind glass, but enclosures are useful for maintaining temperatures and containing the fumes from a print. If you're sticking to printing with standard PLA it's no biggie (though please still ventilate your space), but if you're using other types of filament you may feel more comfortable investing in a printer that comes with an enclosure.

The other downside is that you'll also have to make sure you either cover the printer after use, or dust it regularly.

Other features that are absent from this model include a camera (fun and useful for monitoring your prints while away from home, and something that is notably included on the Creality 3D SparkX i7 Combo) and internal lights.

Neither of these things are deal-breakers and I think newcomers, in particular, will find a lot to love in the Centauri 2 Combo. But if you're serious about 3D printing then you may find yourself quickly considering upgrading to a more capable machine. The Centauri 2 Combo is entering an increasingly crowded 3D printer market — with much of the competition coming from Elegoo itself.

The obvious competitor here is the almost identically titled Elegoo Centauri Carbon 2 Combo. For a mere $70 more right now you can pick that machine up and enjoy a printer with an enclosure, wider filament compatibility, camera and a HEPA + Activated Carbon filter.

Single-color printing with PLA tends to be fairly efficient, when it comes to waste. That changes significantly when you move to multi-color printing, however, because of the way the printer has to switch between colors. That's not a criticism of the Centauri 2 Combo, it's just the nature of the technology.

Basically, when the printer needs to change colors, it retracts the filament being currently used, nips the end off it and ejects the waste, before loading in the new color. If you're printing something with a lot of differently-colored parts (even if you're just using 2 or 3 colors) then it is going to do this a lot. Your waste basked will fill up quickly and if you are concerned about filament wastage (which is difficult to recycle) then printing models with multi-colored parts may not be for you. The picture above is mostly the waste from my two model Daleks!

One way to get around this, if you're printing model kits or things that can be glued together, is by printing each color separately. That will save you both print time and filament wastage, but will not be possible with everything you might want to print.

The Elegoo Centauri 2 Combo is defined by its price point. It's pretty remarkable that you can get a good four-color 3D printer for under over $400 (and sometimes a lot under that price, with Elegoo's occasional discounts). That figure may prove very tempting for those looking to get into printing for the first time.

The price, however, is also behind some of the machine's rougher edges. The lack of an enclosure may not seem like much, but it means that the Centauri 2 Combo really needs to be positioned in a well-ventilated room if you don't want to be inhaling fumes all the time. I can't quibble too much at the lack of a camera or internal lights — at this price, those are nice to have features, rather than essentials.

If you're serious about 3D printing then there are more fully-featured machines out there that may catch your eye. But if you’re new to all this and want a printer that can handle everyday use, as well as more complex prints, it really is hard to find much to grumble about here.

Endor Labs CEO warns AI is making enterprise patching more dangerous

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The pressure to patch vulnerabilities quickly is creating another risk: breaking the systems companies depend on to operate. Only 10% of upgrades or patches could be applied without additional work.

Varun Badhwar, CEO and co-founder of Endor Labs

AI is changing the economics of cybersecurity, but the consequences may not be limited to faster attacks or more sophisticated vulnerability discovery. As enterprises race to secure increasingly complex software environments, the pressure to patch vulnerabilities quickly is creating another risk: breaking the systems companies depend on to operate.

Endor Labs CEO and co-founder Varun Badhwar warned in his recent LinkedIn post that 99% of technology-forward enterprises will experience a breach in the next 12 months, while raising concerns that rushed security updates could trigger operational disruptions of their own. The prediction reflects a growing imbalance between attackers’ ability to exploit vulnerabilities and enterprises’ ability to safely address them.

Badhwar’s concern is not simply that security teams are falling behind. It is that fixing software vulnerabilities has become increasingly difficult to do without introducing new problems.

The scale of vulnerability discovery is changing dramatically. Badhwar points to the fact that the first 100,000 Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs) took approximately 18 and a half years to publish, from the CVE List’s launch in September 1999 until April 2018. In 2026, another 100,000 CVEs were published in just 322 days, crossing that threshold on November 18 with weeks remaining in the year.

For enterprises already managing hundreds of thousands of vulnerabilities, the increase creates a daunting challenge. Attackers only need one successful entry point, while defenders must account for vulnerabilities across an entire environment.

The imbalance is compounded by shrinking exploitation timelines. According to Badhwar, Google Mandiant reported that the mean time to exploit had reached negative seven days, suggesting attackers were increasingly exploiting vulnerabilities before public disclosure by reverse-engineering patches.

Meanwhile, security teams are dealing with remediation timelines that stretch far longer. Badhwar cites Verizon’s 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found remediation times rising to 43 days from 32 days previously, while only 26% of vulnerabilities were fully remediated.

The result is a widening window in which attackers can act before enterprises have completed their response.

The urgency to patch is understandable, but software updates can carry their own operational risks. Enterprises often maintain applications built on years of accumulated dependencies. A security fix may require upgrading components that introduce breaking changes elsewhere in the application.

Badhwar says Endor Labs studied more than 250,000 open vulnerabilities found in open-source dependencies across customer environments, including financial services and technology organizations. The research examined whether teams could move to fixed versions without making additional changes to their applications.

The finding was that only 10% of upgrades or patches could be applied without additional work to address breaking changes. A fix may be available. But implementing it safely can require developers to modify code, test integrations and validate functionality across interconnected systems.

As Badhwar explained, “When teams are rushing one of the easiest things to bypass is testing. These are complex systems, so even minor changes can result in cascading problems.”

That creates a difficult trade-off for security leaders. Moving too slowly leaves vulnerabilities exposed, while moving too quickly can destabilize production environments, disrupt customer-facing services or introduce additional weaknesses.

Badhwar believes AI could help break this cycle, provided it is used to understand software rather than simply generate patches.

AI coding agents are increasingly capable of producing functional code, but functionality does not necessarily translate into secure remediation. In his interview, Badhwar referenced a benchmark in which Fable 5.1 achieved 87% functional code performance but only 37% secure code performance.

He argues that giving AI agents access to application context, including call graphs that show how code interacts with open-source components, can improve their ability to identify and address vulnerabilities without unnecessarily changing the wider application.

Endor Labs’ approach through AURI Fix is designed to evaluate the paths through which vulnerabilities can be reached and exploited, then determine whether the safest remediation involves upgrading a dependency, modifying application code, applying a patched library or replacing a component.

Rather than automatically pushing an extensive software upgrade, the system can make targeted changes to developers’ own code, sometimes involving fewer than 10 lines, to break an exploitable pathway while teams work on a broader fix.

For enterprise leaders, the implications extend beyond vulnerability counts or the number of tickets closed. Badhwar argues that organizations need to measure whether they are actually reducing exposure without sacrificing service availability.

He recommends tracking three indicators: how long internet-facing systems remain exposed to known exploited vulnerabilities, how often emergency fixes cause outages or require rollback, and how quickly critical services can be restored during recovery exercises.

Ultimately, the challenge facing cybersecurity leaders is no longer simply how quickly they can deploy a fix. It is how to make security improvements without creating new operational risks. As AI accelerates both vulnerability discovery and remediation, the ability to prioritize, validate and safely implement changes may become just as important as the speed at which enterprises respond.

For Badhwar, the next frontier of AI in cybersecurity is not generating more vulnerabilities or simply producing patches faster. It is helping enterprises close security gaps without compromising the systems they are trying to protect.

Agon Raises $70M Series A at $450M Valuation to Build Defence AI Lab

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Agon announced on November 23, 2026 that it raised $70 million in Series A funding at a $450 million valuation. Northzone led the round, which will support the company’s effort to build a European defence AI lab supplying sovereign infrastructure and foundation models.

The financing brings Agon’s total capital raised to $100 million, following a $30 million seed round earlier in 2026. Existing investors Lakestar, XYZ, Lux Capital, Global Founders Capital and Speedinvest participated. Unity founder David Helgason, Agon’s chairman and founding investor, increased his investment, while former Sequoia Capital global leader Roelof Botha joined as a new investor.

The UK Sovereign AI Fund and the National Security Strategic Investment Fund (NSSIF) also joined as new investors. The UK Cabinet Office said this was the first direct investment made together by the two government-backed funds, framing it as a way to strengthen sovereign defence capabilities and reduce dependence on overseas infrastructure.

Sovereign AI is Britain’s venture fund dedicated to scaling domestic AI companies, while NSSIF backs strategically important dual-use technologies for national security and defence. The Cabinet Office also connected the investment to highly skilled jobs, industrial renewal and military resilience.

AI Minister Kanishka Narayan linked the backing to the need for defence systems that are “reliable, trustworthy, and deployed responsibly,” saying Agon’s work would contribute to that effort alongside the development of British-made AI for military use.

Agon describes its core technology as a synthetic battle arena powered by the Agon Engine, where developers and defence organisations can train and test autonomous systems against thousands of scenarios before deployment. The environment is intended to provide controlled, repeatable testing against virtual opponents that adapt and draw on live conflict intelligence.

Test cases range from mass drone attacks to disrupted communications, degraded navigation and changing adversary tactics. Agon says simulation complements physical testing by providing training at a scale and speed that cannot be matched in the real world.

An earlier account from XYZ Venture Capital says Agon is beginning with air defence and building its scenario library through design partnerships with developers of defence platforms in Europe and allied countries. XYZ also describes the platform as producing repeatable evidence that procurement officials can use when evaluating systems, connecting simulation work to the documentation behind purchasing decisions.

Agon’s own mission statement adds a governance boundary: enabling decisive advantage in agentic warfare while maintaining human oversight to defend European democratic values.

Agon plans to use the Series A proceeds to expand its engineering and research teams, accelerate development of its platform and foundation models, and deepen work with industry and government customers. Those plans keep the new capital focused on technical development and customer engagement.

Agon is headquartered in London and has a second office in Berlin. Co-founder and CEO Tristam Constant has 20 years of defence experience across the British Army, Anduril and Applied Intuition, where he was most recently Head of European Defence. Co-founder Junaid Hussain founded Cambridge Aerospace and Iconic.

The financing links Agon’s commercial expansion to a broader test of sovereign defence AI: turning simulation tools into deeper industry and government work while retaining the company’s own human-oversight commitment and meeting the reliability expectations of its public backers.

Coprod Raises $6M Pre-Seed to Bring Modular Robot Hardware to Market

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Swiss robotics startup coprod announced a $6 million pre-seed round on November 23, 2026, to fund its first commercial component line and a prototyping facility in Lausanne. The company is developing modular hardware and software intended to reduce the time and cost of building robots for specific tasks.

Founderful and PROTOTYPE led the round, with participation from General Catalyst, Pace Ventures, Tiny Supercomputer Investment Company, Kima Ventures, SDAC, Unruly Capital and Venturekick. Angel investors included Hugging Face founder Thomas Wolf, the Dassault Family and Florent Perrichon.

Coprod says custom task-specific robot development has traditionally required large teams of domain experts and two to three years of work, while its stack enables a small team to do the same work in a few months for $5,000 to $25,000 per design iteration.

“A robot with purpose-built hardware designed for a specific task will do that task faster, cheaper, and more reliably than any general-purpose robot ever could,” said CEO and co-founder Loïc Delineau.

The company’s co:system combines sensing, motion, power and battery modules on one EtherCAT bus, with centralized real-time control and a common developer API. Its software pairs a C++ core with Python bindings, while distributed clocks and hardware timestamping coordinate connected devices. Published specifications list a 100 Mb/s bus, a typical 1 kHz control loop and jitter, or timing variation, below 20 microseconds.

The hardware can compose into a complete robot or integrate separately into an existing one. Coprod designs, builds and tests it in Switzerland.

The Lausanne facility will house CNC lathes, mills, lasers and electronic testing equipment. Coprod says the workshop is designed to shorten development cycles from two weeks to 24 hours while producing datasheets and digital-twin models for each component.

EPFL graduates Delineau, Vincent Nguyen and Matthias Schuller formed coprod in late 2024, initially building a bipedal demonstrator in a garage in the Swiss Alps. In an April 2, 2026 Venture Kick interview, the founders said the co:dodo robot became operational shortly before Christmas 2025 after about three months of evening and weekend work.

The founders said in that interview that their earlier CHF 40,000 Stage 2 award from Venture Kick would support market outreach, industry fairs and marketing, as well as a move from the garage into a dedicated workspace.

Coprod says its components are already used in Swiss robots, including Switzerland’s first student-built humanoid from the ETH Robotics Club, which has taken its first steps in Zurich. The company is establishing partnerships with robotics integrators and laboratories to validate prototypes ahead of a commercial launch planned for early 2027.

By the end of 2027, coprod plans to have its entire component line qualified, certified and on the market. Large-scale production would take place in Shenzhen and Texas to provide regional supply chains, while its headquarters, core team and research and development remain in Switzerland.

An April 17, 2026 analysis by McKinsey & Company estimates that actuators account for 40% to 60% of a humanoid robot’s component costs, compared with 10% to 20% for sensing and perception systems and 10% to 15% for compute and control platforms.

The analysis describes the industry as premodular, with many robot makers vertically integrating or closely codeveloping components because standardized, high-volume supplier ecosystems are not yet available for several critical parts.

It identifies predictable multiyear volumes, stable subsystem architectures and converging interface standards as the conditions for supplier-led modularization at scale, and says none is fully present.

That leaves coprod with a commercialization challenge alongside its manufacturing plans: its modular approach requires robotics teams to adopt shared components in a supply chain that still lacks predictable multiyear volumes, stable architectures and converging interface standards.

A 17-Million-Line Proof Says Yes. Here Is What It

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On November 20, 2026, Levent Alpöge released a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis, written with Claude, as a Lean library of about 17 million lines. A day later the Lean and Mathlib communities certified both its statement and its proof. Clay's prize still needs publication and time, but as a machine-checked result the question is answered: every nontrivial zero of ζ(s) lies on the line Re(s) = ½.

A proof is a finite object. Lean does not walk the zeros one by one. It checks that every step follows from the last. So this post is not a complaint about size. It asks a different question, one a proof does not answer by itself:

If a zero had left the line, how long would it take to notice?

Map each zero ρ through w = (ρ − 1)/ρ. On the critical line |w| = 1 exactly. The functional equation pairs a zero at σ + it with one at (1 − σ) + it, and their images obey

So if a zero sits at σ > ½, its image falls inside the unit circle and its twin falls outside. In Li's criterion, λₙ = Σ [1 − (1 − 1/ρ)ⁿ], the inside one fades and the outside one grows like |w′|ⁿ. For large t,

The twin's growth eventually pushes some λₙ below zero. That negative value is the signature of an off-line zero.

Growth of Δ/t² per step is very slow, and the twin has to beat something: the on-line zeros, whose combined contribution itself grows as (n/2)(ln n − 1 + γ − ln 2π). Setting the twin equal to that background gives the threshold:

n_crit · Δ / t² ≈ ln n_crit + ln(ln n_crit + c) − ln 4

So the number of Li coefficients you need scales as t² log t / Δ. The t²/Δ part was known (Oesterlé 2000; Voros 2017). The log factor matters, because it means the "constant" in front is not constant. An earlier version of my own paper got this wrong: it claimed a universal value of 29.93. That value fails at every height I tested.

I built λₙ from the first 200 actual zeros of ζ plus the known density of zeros above them. I checked it against the exact λ₁…λ₅, then inserted one hypothetical off-line pair and scanned for the first negative λₙ:

At t = 10¹², the same formula needs about 6 × 10²⁶ coefficients.

The core of the model fits in a few lines:

Here is the part I find interesting. Nothing in that threshold uses the primes. It depends on two things only: the pairing (|w|·|w′| = 1) and how fast the background grows. For L-functions of conductor q, the cost barely moves. On real zeros of L(s, χ₋₄) the shift in n·Δ/t² is about +0.15, not ln 4.

The off-line zeros above were inserted by hand. Some functions don't need that. Davenport and Heilbronn built Dirichlet series with the same kind of functional equation as ζ but no Euler product, and their zeros genuinely wander off the line. I took the partner function mod 5. Its strongest off-line zero sits at 2.3086 + 8.9184i.

Before computing anything exact, I wrote down the prediction: the background for conductor 5, plus the twins of its off-line zeros. Then I computed its Li coefficients exactly, from Taylor coefficients of log Λ around s = 1. That method uses no zeros at all, and two independent runs agree to 10⁻⁸.

No primes in the input, a real off-line zero, and the crossing lands exactly where the pairing says it should.

So the cost of seeing is geometry, and the reason nothing is there to see is arithmetic. The Davenport–Heilbronn function has the geometry without the arithmetic, and its zeros leave the line. The proof settles the second. The threshold describes the first: how far out a twin would have to be, at a given height, before a sign test could see it.

Both kinds of result belong in mathematics. A proof tells you that. A measured invariant tells you how hard it is to look.

Paper, code and data: 10.5281/zenodo.24447722
